THE CORSAIR AND HIS CONQUEROR. 


CHAPTER I. 


THE marvellous reports bruited about among 
travelling Englishmen, as to the climate of Algiers, 
with regard to its influence on chest complaints, had 
the effect of shortening my extremely happy séjour 
among the classic mountains of Provence. It was 
impossible to rest quietly in the otherwise salutary 
little town of Hyéres, and reflect, each time I sean- 
ned the blue horizon of the Mediterranean, that a 
reputed panacea lay beyond, on a shore full of all 
that could give interest to the study of races, of 
whom the record of centuries has failed*in esighifh- 
ing more than a mere proof of their .mysterious and 
terrible existence. Simple curiosity would have been 
in any case a stimulus; and accordingly I was not 
surprised to find myself in the coupé of the Diligence 
picking owt-its slow route amid the seaward hills, 
that have re-ecBoed, time gone by, to the impassioned

strains of the Troubadgurs. The temperature, as we" 
‘reached after a, seven * hours’ journey the painted 
Bastides on th8 outskirte of Marseilles, was sensibly 
cooler, and the general aspect of things around de- 
cidedly more wintry ; 3 while heavy rain, ishering i in 
a clouded and starless evening, proved no pleasant , 
companion to a solitary tourtst. I was glad, there; 
fore, to take advantage even of the'damp hospitality 
afforded by the sheets of an Hotel near the Canne- 
biére, auguring, from the undiminished patter on 
the window panes, melancholy omens for the com- 
mencement of the morrow’s journey. 

A bright and cloudless day, however, put an, 
end to my forebodings, as I got on board the screw : 
steamer “Zouave,” that was moored off La J oliette, | 
the largest harbour of Marseilles. It was the begin- 
ning of February, a season during which, in our own 
climate, one would have been “thankful for a single 
"ray of the brilliant sun that was glowing so intensely 
above us. I soon found, however, that the Mistral,* 

, that terrible scourge of Southern France, was sweep- 
ing with irresistible fury over the town, rendering 
of*a still deeper hue the cerulean above, and forcing 
the smallest intruding cloudlet to fly before its im- 
petuous breath. Among the tall masts and taper 
yards it bellowed forth a varying gamut, fantastic

and rolled madly among the big waves, like a giddy 
coquette dancing to the orchestra of the boisterous 
Mistral. 
The Chateau D’If, which stands on a barren rock 
a little way out of the port, had a sad and gloomy 
aspect as we passed, dhd I amused myself by trying 
to imagine the peculiar sénsations experienced by 
the héro of Dumas’ “ Monte Christo,” at that terrible 
moment when he found himself dragged down in 
his canvas shroud, beneath the yielding wave. The 
view of the receding French coast, after a little time, 
was extremely grand, the heights of Marseilles blond- 
ing with the more inland mountains, and forming an 
irregular and picturesque outline, filled up with a 
coloring of bluish grey. The subject of sea-sicky-ss 
may be let alone; but at the same-time, I Guld not 
help remarking that the passengers—mostly French-

To those waverers on board who, though sick at 
heart, aye yet willing for the sake of appearances ~ 
to sit down at table with the rest, and endeavor t6 
swallow a trifle or two, calculating at the same time 
the exact number of minutes that must elapse before 
they make a rush from the saloon, the dinner that 
avas served up to us would certainly have proved a 
settler. Cutlets done with bread-crumbs and indiffe- 
rent butter, fish reeking with oil, jugged hare with 
the most unctuous sauce that could possibly be ims 
gined, and soup with a stratum of yellow grease, are 
dishes by no means calculated to provoke appetite, 
especially if the stomach be qualmish. Added to 
this, a most villianous odour of bilge water pervaded 
the whole cabin, and seemed to impart its peculiar 
taste to every dish that came to table, while-the 
windows and ports 3vere hermetically sealed @ la 
Frangaise, to prevent the ,intrusion of the slightest 
current of fresh air. The food on board this and 
the other Mediterranean lines is paid for beforehand, 
simultaneously with the fare, and it really seems as 
if there were some diabolical league between the 
steward and owners of the vessel, for the purpdse 
of putting the boldest appetites hors-de-combat, and 
thus bringing an equal ratio of profit to the pockets 
of the Companies. Certain it is that few peonle 
would care to lay out money on such greasyymesti- 
bles, and it seems very hard that so many sufferers 
should be forced, will or nill, to pay for articles they 
find impossible to devour. 

The storm, as we got into the Gulf of. Lyons, in- 
creased in vehemence, and the vessel rolled so muck: 
that it was almost imspossible to rest ir- one’s berth.

Although the ports were closely fastened, yet the 
water oozed thrqugh in such a manner that,I had to 
‘wrap myself tightly in &@ blanket to keep at all dry. 
When I came on fleck the next morning, I found 
that our mainsail had been torn in two or three 
places by the wind, and we had been driven so far 
out of our course as to be riding off Barcelona. To 
confess the truth, I was by no means sorry for this, 
as the loss of a few hours wag amply repaid by a 
sight of the large city gleaming in the early morning 
sun, with the rugged and majestic mountains rising 
grandly in its rear. As the day wore on, we were 
enabled to stand out to sea again, and passed between 
the Balearic Islands at night. The next day there 
was a wonderful change in the temperature, and the 
wind had settled down into a dead calm. Thé air 
was deliciously soft, and so powerful were the rays 
of the afternoon sun, that I was forced to seek the 
partial shade of the companion-hatchway. At six 
that evening we sighted the dim coast-line of Africa, 
and the Scirocco that was blowing thence, made itself 
apparent by a tiny warm breath that lazily fanned my 
cliéek. As the night advanced, we were enveloped 
for upwards of three hours in a fog, which completely 
put us out of our reckoning; so deeming that there 
was little chance of arriving at our destination till 
the day, I retired as usual to bed. At about 
five o’clock in the morning, I was awoke by the noise 
of quick tramping overhead, and passengers, with 
their faces protruding from the cabin doors, bawling — 
and shouting to each other. I jumped up, and find- 
ang that the incessant vibration of the crew had 
ceased, dressed hastily, and moynted“on deck. The

night was*still hanging over us, studded with num- 
berless constellations of suclt intense brilliancy that — 
they seemed like o series of, electri¢ Aights gleaming~ 
in the firmament; while the morning star threw so 
bright a*wake upon the waters, hat, ltd I viewed it 
from my cabin window, I should easily have mistaken 
it for the reflection of the moon. We were at anchor 
in a vast bay, encircled by immense mountains, that 
loomed indistinct on all sides, and awaiting the arri- 
val of the health-beat, which soon skimmed over the 
water like a will-o’-the-wisp, conspicuous from a 
distance by the glitter of its green lantern. Ina 
short time, a faint line of rose-colored light streaked 
the horizon, and with wonderful rapidity grew wider 
and wider, changing, in the most superb gradations, 
to.a bright carmine, and then a fiery red. Then, 
from his watery bed, burst forth the first African sun 
I had beheld, and infan instant it was broad daylight. 
To the left were the etermfally snow-clad peaks of the 
Jurjura, bathed in a flood of crimson glory, with 
clouds floating like fleeces of gold beneath them, 
while to the right stretched an undulating mountain- 
line of the softest emerald green. And beforeme, 
kissed by the rippling waters of that now blue sea, 
lay the once terrible Algiers,—a tigress sated with 
the gore of centuries. In tier after tier of dazzling 
white rose the Corsair city, to the very sumpyi of 
the hill on whose incline it is built, bearittf a strong 
resemblance to a snowdrift cast thither by the vio- 
lenge of some mighty wind. And an ineffable still- 
ness seemed to pervade it in the early morning, like 
the unkvoken quiet that, according td Mussulman 
credence, hasgs eternally o’er the mysterious desert

* The desert town here alluded to is mentioned by Sale as having 
been built by Shedad, son of Ad, who spared neither time nor money 
fo convert it into the, most magnificent city on the earth. His palace, 
‘was a perfect master-piece of art and luxury, and was enclosed by deli- 
cious gardens, which Shedad impiously.designated as his earthly Para- 
dise, seeking thereby to be worshipped as a Deity. The inhabitants, in 
course of time, having turned from the service of the true God to follow 
idolatry, Divine wrath overtook them, and a deadly wind called Sarsar, 
blowing for seven days and nights, utterly destroyed them all, save the 
prophet Houd and some of his followers, who had in vain exhorted the 
people to repentance. To complete the legend, the city (Mussulmen 
affirm) still exists in the desert of Aden, though invisible to the gene- 
rality of mankind. Providence, however, permits it to be seen occa- 
sionally, and a certain Colabah related to the Caliph Moawiyah, that, 
having one day sought a stray camel, he found himself suddenly atthe 
gates of a large city, and having entered therein and walked through 
some of the streets, without encountering,s single inhabitant, fled in 
terror; taking care, however, to bring away some curiously carved 

“stones, which he shewed to the Cfliph as a proof of his gdventure. 
Allusion is made to the fate of this city, in the eighty-ninth chapter of 
the Koran, entitled “The Day-break.”

CYAPTER II. 


A RESPECTABLE portion of the spirit of their an- 
cestors appears yet to survive in the tawny breasts 
of the Algerines. Adulterated though it doubtless 
be with the waters of civilization, yet there seems 
quite enough of the original blood for the creation 
of ds good a set of pirates as one would wish to 
encounter on a foggy night, at sea. Such at least, 
were my thoughts as our ship was surrounded with . 
about a dozen boats, manned by as many sturdy 
Arabs, who, with their tanned faces, and sinewy 
arms and legs, seemed the very personifications of 
savage brute force. I was assailed by three or four 
young giants, bawling at me in the guttural toffes 
of the Arabic, and almost pulling me to pieces in 
their frantic attempts to inveigle me into their respec- 
tive craft. Having happily succeeded in keeping 
my senses and portmanteau in their fightplac&, I 
jumped into the first boat that came to hand, and 
was instantly rowed away, amid a volley of treble 
“h’s?? and quadruple “k’s,” and other wonderful 
expectorating noises that followed me in {hg shape of 
Mohamn®dan oaths. Under such trying circume 
stances it was not upnatural that I shoald inwardly

deplore my ignorance ef their language, which pre- 
vented my calfying the tempers of my disappointed 
assailants, in some such*sentiments as the following ; 
“Gentlemen, I grigve that you should all fhree have 
‘set your hearts on conducting me home; were such 
a thing possible, I would with pleasure divide myself, 
and jump into all your boats at once, to please you, 
but, as I am’subject to the common laws of humanity, 
I fear I must content myself with one.” 

The landing was even worse than the attack on 
board. A crowd of Arabs were waiting for me at 
the place of debarkation, and closed round me the 
moment I set my foot on shore. I was unfortunate 
enough to have two or three hand parcels, which 
were instantly snatched from me, resistance being 
out of the question, and, in company with my port- 
manteau, were marched up a, flight of stairs, and 
through a fine old Moorish gateway surmounted by 
the arms of the Deys, which I would have given 
worlds to stop and examine. Where the fair sex 
‘are concerned, the landing at Algiers is anything but 
a pleasant operation, and an English lady, a friend 
of*mine, actually took a standing jump of at least 
three feet, in order to rid herself of the clawing from 
those dirty hands. Of course the boatman was dis- 
satisfied with his fare,—who ever heard of a boatman 
in Ymyegart ®f the world that was not?—and, as I 
had no time to dispute, I fiung a coin extra into his 
hands, and rushed after my luggage. Happily my 
persecutors allowed me the option of choosing an 
hotel, and, finding myself just opposite the door of 
dhe “Porte de France,” I entered therein? and was 
shewn into ® room on the quatziéme, that it would

have even ventured so far as He it, in that 
respect, on an equality gvith Madeira or Egypt, 
assuming a too hasty deduction by mere comparison 
of the respective mean temperatures, but without 
the slightest regard to the conditions under which 
those thermometrical figures may have been evolved. 
Without, however, endeavouring to disparage its 
presumed suitability as a winter residence for invalids, 
it is but fair to sta%& that drawbacks exist, which, in 
the eyes of many medical men, would be sufficient 
of themselves to counterbalance the otherwise genial 
character of its atmosphere, and induce valetudina- 
rians to turn their attention to other regions, equally 
beneficent, in which those drawbacks do not exist. 
To patients in very precarious health it is not too 
much to say that no worse place could be chosen than 
Algiers; while to those who, having alréady passed 
one or two winters in*the South are progressing 
favorably, it may be rendered, with o few trifling 
attentions as to residence and situation, not only 
highly beneficial, but even more invigorating than, 
most of those places in which chest invalids jake 
refuge from the rigor of a more northerly winter. 
Under any circumstances the valetudinarian should 
not think of visiting Algiers till the end of January, 
about which time the fine weather commencesy The 
most advisable way would be to spend thé preceding 
winter months in the south of France, which is gene-,

by that means the invalid will have enjoyed the

the Barbary coast. i rains of the wintry solstice 
, make their app ce about the end of Octgber, and 
last without i sa Poilasions throughout the months of 
November, Decembgr, and January, uttgly pre- 
cluding out-door exercise to all but the strong. For 
days, and sometimes weeks, they pour down in 
drenching, unpitying torrents, as though the water 
was discharged en masse from the clouds, and it is 
only at rare intervals that the | sun can shew his 
cheering face. The invalid can? if he please, walk 
from one end of the town to the other, under the 
shelter of the French arcades, without receiving a 
‘drop of moisture on his clothes, but at the same 
time they are, as may be imagined, so crowded, 
that the air within them seems by no means desirable 
for respiration. During the intervals, however, be- 
tween the rains the atmosphere | is the most balmy 
and delicious that can well be imagined. Not a par- 
ticle of damp can be felt, and the ordinary heat 
having been tempered by the preceding showers, it 
ig most soothing and invigorating to diseased lungs. 
» The ground too, soon dries up, and in the course of 
an dour or two, under the influence of a brilliant 
sun and a deep blue sky, one would almost be 
tempted to forget that the rain was ever a visitor to 
these fertile shores. Some have endeavoured to ac- 
countefor the centinuation of the wet weather by 
assuming hat the snow-clad peaks of the greater 
~Atlas, which are 12,000 feet above the level of the 
sea, intercept the clouds from the North, which, as 
a matter of Course, discharge their accumulated 
mofsture upon the Tell, or portion of land r@aching 
to the sea shorg; while others aggin hive asserted 


AND HIS CONQUEROR, 15 


that the vapors arising from, the Atlantic are borne 
eastwaryl by the west winds, and ultjmately converted » 
into rain. The latter theery seems to be weakenejl, 
howevas, by the fact that at On less rain falls than 
at Algiers, and less at Algiers than at Tunis, which 
would be directly in opposition to the result we 
should expect, supposing such were the case. In 
February the rain ceases, although there are occasion- 
ally a few wet days, and the temperature during the 
month is as delightful as that of the latter end of an 
English May. March presents a succession of warm, 
dry days, and in April the heat begins to be now 
and then oppressive, and continues on the increase. 
At the beginning of May most of the valetudinarians 
take their departure, and from thence to the end of 
September the heat of the city is most trying to 
unclimatised Europeans, vegetation being dried up 
by the powerful rays of the sun. The absence of 
rain therefore, in ,the summer months, is as much 
felt as its continued presence in the winter. Accor- 
ding to the nineteen years experience of Maccarthy, 
the average depth of rain that falls in Algiers fronr 
the Ist October to the Ist June, is 76 centimetres, 
while for the remaining months it is but 17.*

and the author is a member of the Historical Society of Algiers, the 
Archeological Society of Constantine, &c. besides having been em* 
ployed since 1849, by the Minister of War, on an exploring expedition 
over the Algerine Territory.

The town itself looks almost due East, and the 
knowledge of this fact will be of great servicp to the 
valetudinarian, ‘whose apartments should look, if 
possible, to the Soutl, Even when kept in goors by 
the rain, theré are very few days on which he will 
not be able to sit with his window open, as the at- 
mosphere is rarely infected with damp, even in the 
heaviest showers, as is the case in England. The 
coldest month is December, and eyen then it is rarely 
that the. thermometer reaches the freezing point. 
The following is a table of the winter months in 
Algiers, the temperature being pretty similar along 
the coast from Oran to Tunis.

, From these figures, then, it will be evident that 
the mean temperature for the six winter months, 
viz:from October to March, lies between 59° and 
60°. Towards the end of April the heat commences 
to be what Englishmen are apt to term “sweltering,” 
and I found it impossible to take the slightest exer- 
cise without profuse perspiration. At a season like 
this, one looks languidly at the Jurjura, and caleu- 
dates how many delicious fruit-creams might be frozen 
with a few cartloads of that eternal snow! Even-the 
early matutinal air affords little coolness, and I shall 
notteasily fotgét one morning on which the Seirocco 
was prevalent, the thermometer actually® indicating

75° at the Sing of five! June, July, August, and 
September, are the hottest months, the highest mean 
temperature of each being yspectively 85°, 86°, 92°, 
and 88°, which are not, howevgr, by any means so 
high as one might expect to find in tlose latitudes. 
Nay, the summer months of our own climate have 
snot unfrequently marked higher figures, and hence 
the climate of Algiers may justly be termed equable, 
the seasons seeming to glide one into another with 
an almost imperceftible difference. As a general 
rule the hottest period of the day is between nine 
and eleven, a. m., before the sea-breeze sprifigs up, 
and the most dangerous time for the invalid is sun- 
set, on account of the noxious dews which accompany 
the descent of the orb of day. When the darkness 
has fairly set in, however, he may venture out with 
impunity, and a promenade i in the mild evening air 
has even been recothmended by some of the French 
doctors. 

Algiers is comparatively free from the cutting 
blasts which sweep over the greater part of Southern 
Europe during the months of February and March. 
The most obnoxious wind with which it is visited is 
the Scirocco, termed by the Arabs Arifi, which takes 
its rise from among the vast sands of the rearward 
Sahara, and blows steadfastly for two or three days at 
a time.* The thermometer is raised at leart ten

There is a west wind which blows at times, but 
without much impetuosity, and there is also an east 
wind, which, whenever it occurs, (which is but rarely) 
fills the air with moisture, and is therefore injurious 
to invalids. Storms are by no means frequent in the

But the greatest drawback to the invalid is the dust.

The greatest mistake an invalid can make is to 
take up his residence in the town. There are two 
suburbs, Mustapha and St. Eugene, of easy access, 
which afford charming situations, both with regard 
to scenery, and that more important consideration, 
good air. Mustapha is the warmer suburb -of, the 
two, but for those who can bear a little bracing, St. 
Eugene is decidedly preferable. The latter place is 
,& favorite resort for English valetudinarians, who 
preder it for the sake of the refreshing sea-breezes, 
which offer a delightful contrast to the occasionally 
overpowering heat of the town. Mustapha, too, 
although warmer, has a decidedly damper soil than 
St. Eugene, and is much more open to the dust, 
from which nuisance the latter is almost entirely free.

The Neighbourhood of St. Eugene was much patron- 
ized by the Algerine nobility before the conquest, 
and the hill sides on the way thither were at one

The market of Algiers produces very good soles, 
mullet, Jolin Dories, sardines and lampreys, besides 
a host of other fish peculiar to the African waters. 
The oysters, however, are of very inferior quality, 
and the lobsters are too large &nd coarse for food, 
but a species of cray-fish, denominated by the French 
“ Langouste,” afford an excellent substitute. Those 
who wish to obtain reasonable returns for their money, 
should offer the salesman little more than half the 
sum he demands, to which, especially in the case of 
a regular customer, he will generally accede. To 
those who may have a “penchant” for outlandish food 
morsels of sharks’ flesh are sometfmes exposed for sale, 
together with basketsful of ‘mammoth snails and tor- 
toises,—delicacies, however, for which a high frice 
must be*paid. Strawberries are eaten at the end of

every year, and artichokes, lettuce and endive, are 
abundant from January. Walnuts are plentiful

That singular reptile, the Chameleon, may be 
purchased occasionally in the mdfket. It has a long 
thin body and a large head, with the most hideous 
mouth that can well be conceived, while its eyes—like 
diminutive brilliants set in globes of green leather— 
protrude from their sockets, and have the singular 
power of looking different ways at once, so that while; 
one optic may be apparently directed to an object in) 
front, the other turns slyly to see that no treason is 
‘going on behind. Its four fect are jointed in the 
manner of the human arni, each foot being termi- 
nated with three long claws, like fingers, with Which 
it slowly picks its journey. Its method of locomotion 
is extremely quaint and amusing, especially in climb- 
ing trees. First it gives a careful glance round to 
see that all is safe, and then slowly advances its front 
leg, opening the claws like an elderly gentleman 
about to shake hands. Having grasped a twig, it 
pauses for another moment, looks round again, and 
then seizes the twig with the other claw. The same 
operation is performed with the two hind legs, till it 
rests altogether on the twig, around which it curls its 
tail, as it looks carefully about for another step in its 
singular pfogress. The original idea of its subsisting 
entirely on air has now given place *o the more 


AND H&S CONQUEROR. 25 


rational theory of small flies or aérial animalculse, 
which, by quickly darting out its long tongue, it is 
enabled to entrap. That it has the power of changing, 
to blue or white is also an undoubted exaggeration, 
but, at the same time, it is capable 8f materially 
altering its shade from a greenish yellow to a dark 
green, according to the dark gr light color of the 
object which it may wish to resemble. I placed a 
chameleon, one day, on a table strewn with pale 
colored leaves, and %emarked, as it picked its way 
along, that it was of a color almost similar to the 
vegetation. I produced a red silk handkerchief, with 
which I partially covered it, and was surprised to 
see that in an instant it had altered its hue to the 
darkest imaginable shade, while, on again removing 
the handkerchief, it immediately assumed its former 
pale yellow tinge. It is clearly apparent, therefore, 
that this wondrous gift is bestowed on it (like the 
footid stench which the “skunk” is said to emit, or 
the intky fluid of the scuttle fish) for the purpose of 
self-protection, in order that by assaming as nearly , 
as possible the shade of surrounding objects it might 
thus be enabled, in almost every instance, to baflle 
the pursuit and escape the detection of an enemy, 
however powerful and vigilant.*

The promotion of emigration to their new colony 
is at the present day the most favorite hobby of thé 
French nation. To further this object the Emperor 
has announced it as his design to render Algiers, as 
much as possible, like a “petit Paris,”—and to a 
certain degree he has succeeded. The lower portion 
of the town is almost entirely modern, and there are 
vaulted arcades on both sides the Bab-a-zoun and 
Bab-el-oued streets in the style of many of the 
Parisian thoroughfares.- These streets rp broad. 
enough t? admit of the passage of two carriages 
abreast, and, in fact, it is only in the lewer portion

On the spot occupied two years ago by the Djenina, or

are numerous stone seats that command a fine view 
of the Bay. These seats are occupied not only during 
the day, but also in the delicious warmth of the eve- 
ning, by representatives of the European and African 
races intent on enjoying the spiced breezes and the 
magnificent view. There, too, a speculative French- 
man has set up a gigantic Camera Obscura, wherein, 
for a small gratuity, the most wondrous panorama 
of the town and its environs can be seen. The vivid 
blue of the sky, the folds of the native garments, and 
every crested ripple on the laughing sea, are painted 
so exquisitely upon the whitened disc by the moving 
pencil of Nature herself, that_it seems as if a tan- 
gible vision of beauty were presented to the eye,— 
dispelled, however, in an instant, by the admission 
of the light of day, and the consequent blankness 
of that magic pasteboard table. In the centre of

About a mile from the other extremity of the town 
is another public garden, called the Jardin d’Essai, 
and instituted, as its name implies, for the purposes 
of experimental agriculture. Inj it there is an avenue 
of palm trees about eight hundfed yards in length, 
the cluster of dates hanging down rich and plentiful 
from the immense leaves. The trees are of a very 
fair size now in some places, and after the growth of 
a few years the avenue will be magnificent. A hot- 
house has been erected in these gardens, where they 
can rear tropical plants to an immense size. One 
would almost as soon expect to find a refrigerator at 
the North Pole as a hothouse’in Africa. The gar- 
dens are very extensive, and comprise whole fields of 
different plants, as the banana and sugar cane, Which 
ehave answered every expectation. They have endea- 
vored, too, with great success, to rear the cochineal ; 
besides which, tea, indigo, rice and the India rubber 
plant have been tried with very fair results. Within 
an enclosure were some ostriches in a very playful

From the town itself to various points in the

On returning to the town one is surprised at the

of money have been expended in the way of decora-

the great and only object of the proprietors. It is 
quite lamentable to think how many fine Moorish 
houses have been pulled down to give place to these 
tasteless five and six storied edifices, which a tolerably 
strong earthquake would soon lay in ruins. But the 
vandalism that has been exhibited in Algiers is per- 
fectly startling, the finest specimens of architecture 
having been pulled down as ruthlessly as if they 
were so many molehills erfcumbering the surface of 
the earth. And in exchange for what? Ugly lodging 
houses with green blinds, or these vapid cafés, in 
which cards are played and Absinthe drunk the 
whole day long. Neither war, nor famine, nor pes- 
tilence, are so destructive in their effects as that 
terrible decoction of wormwood.* Like smoking, it

“When Adam had planted the vine Satan watered

lion. And when it is at its height, like the fourth 
animal, he falls down, wallows, extends himself, 
and—goes to sleep!”

A short extract from my first day’s diary in Man- 
ritania may not be devoid of interest to the would-be 
tourist :—Got into bed. Slept. Dreamt. A horrible 
sound of triumph—a war note—rings in my ear. Not 
loud and bombastic, but on the contrary, low, smooth, 
and scarce perceptible—the very ghost of a sound— 
and yet so monotonous, so terribly monotonous, that 
I stop my ears, and run wildly to and fro to escape 
it. Enter a figure from the bowels of the earth, or 
the clou‘ls, or any other absurd place—one*musn’t be 
particular in“dream-land—who answers, probably, fo

the name of Achilles, Of course I run against him 
in my frenzy. The god-like hero pauses, poises his 
,Spear, takes careful. aim, and hits me exactly on the 
“tip of my nose. Great Zeus, how it emprts! It 
really won’t*do to be insulted by a fellow who, after 
all, is little more than a myth. So on him with 
“might and main, doubling my fists, like a true 
Englishman. If he only were mortal how he’d 
feel those smashing blows! How I go in with my 
left and right, reckless—almost Blind with fury—and 
how little heeds he my wrath, as he wounds me on my 
twin eyelids, and then on my cheek. Confusion—this 
won’t do! One last desperate blow, and—I awake, 
and find myself battling with air. Still continues 
that diabolical war note, and doubling stinging is 
the smart of those preternatural wounds. I jump 
up wildly, and light a candle. With its gleam, 
ceases that agonizing buzz—A’chilles has mizzled— 
got under the bed or out ‘vf window, search, under 
the circumstances, being evidently useless. But six 
.gossamer mites flit past me and settle on the wall. 
I get out of bed—procure a damp towel—and, with 
owe fell swoop, half a dozen Mosquitoes are dissolved 
into eternal smash! Greedy, like all else in this 
Pirate city, of human blood, if one may judge from 
the quantity on the towel. Next morning, look in 
the glass—promising cherry at end of nose—eyelids 
will scarcely open—wouldn’t swear to my own face— 
but must, at all events, abjure photographic likenesses 
of myself, for a week at least.

CHAPTER IT. 


To turn from the modern quarter of Algiers, which 
forms but a fraction of the old town, and wander 
among the old Moorish streets, through which the 
stream of civilization has not yet rolled, is equivalent 
to taking a jump, at least a couple of centuries deep, 
into the abyss of bye-gone ages. One has a dim 
consciousness of having stepped from the New world 
into the precincts of the Old, and the change could 
scarcely be more sudden, than if it were possible to 
stride from Paris to Mauritania in the fabled seven- 
leagued boots of yore. , 

The city of Algiers is built on the incline of a hill 
one hundred and forty-three metres in height, the la- 
borious steepness of which is in some way alleviated 
by the long steps in which the streets are paved, and 
which naturally gives them the appearance of narrow 
staircases, with high white walls rising on each side. 
In most cases buttresses project from the houses, 
which, meeting those of the opposite edifices, protect 
the route beneath from the languid influence of the 
Scirocco, or the incessant rains of winter, and the 
shade soformed is at times extremely grateful. These 
buttresses sedin to be propped up by means of” thick 


44 THE CORSAIR 


round pieces of wood, rbout two feet in length, which 
issue at an acute angle from the wall of the lower 
story; and an admirable contingency is formed 
against the ravages of an earthquake by the mutual 
support which the contact of the overhanging projec- 
tions afford to the houses. In many instances an 
arching of solid masonry, so low that I have been 
often obliged to bend my head while passing beneath 
it, renders the street little better than a dark vault, 
the funereal aspect of which, is,‘in some measure, re- 
lieved by the small and curiously carved house doors 
on each side,—the sole indicators of the proximity of 
life. So absurdly diminutive are these portals that 
the inhabitants are obliged to effect an ingress or 
egress therefrom in a style somewhat similar to that 
in which Harlequin performs his transit through the 
moon, or the yielding window of a sausage shop, 
a fugitive from the practical jokes of the untiring 
Clown. Taking into consideration the tallness of 
stature which invariably characterises the native races 
of Barbary the disproportion of these microscopic, 
and consequently, difficult entrances, may afford, at 
first sight, just cause for astonishment. The grounds 
for surprise, however, will vanish, when we reflect 
that but a few years ago Power was wielded by the 
hand of Might, and few men’s houses were secure 
from an occasiznal invasion by their stronger neigh- 
bours, it being by no means an uncommon circum- 
stance, during the Ottoman domination, for a Turk 
to enter the threshold of a Moor, and lay his hand 
on the first object which struck his fancy, deeming it, 
at the same time, an act of forbearance if hu omitted 
to offér violence to the women of the household. In

most cases the streets are as narrow as they are occa- 
sionally low, many of them which I took the trouble 
of measuring not being more than thirty-five to forty , 
inches in, width. So great was the jealously of the 
Deys that ponderous gates, closed at certain periods 
of the night, kept the inhabitants of one quarter from 
communication with those of another,—a by no means 
despicable precaution in times when a revolt, accom- 
panied by a bow stringing of his highness, was thought 
of scarcely greater nfoment than a change of ministry 
in our own favored island. These gates remained 
in their respective sockets for a few years after the 
French conquest, but were ultimately removed by 
order of the Duc de Rovigo. 

It is a pleasant way of passing half an hour to 
find oneself fixed in the middle of one of these streets 
between two obstacles which only time and patience 
can remove. Such was once my position. A mule, 
loaded with an immense bundle of sticks which com- 
pletely obstructed the thoroughfare, was behind me, 
while another, laden with a similar burden, suddenly, 
made its appearance in front. Seeing that a collision 
must inevitably take place I sought the protectign 
of a narrow doorway, making myself as small, under 
the circumstances, as I conveniently could. The mules 
met, as fortune would have it, exactly before me, and 
the drivers swore at each other in gutturals for some 
time, neither being willing to retreat or to seek, for 
mutual convenience, the outlet of a rearward street. 
And it will scarcely be credited that, rather than 
give up a single foot of ground, they coolly and 
deliberately unloaded their mules, placifig the sticks 
on the grovhd beside them, which operation beitig

finished the two poo- animals were squeezed past 
each other and reloaded, the drivers enjcying the 
full and happy consciousness of not having budged 
a single inch to accommodate each other! 

To ascend three or four of these streets in succes- 
sion is fatiguing work, even for a tolerably strong 
man. To descend them is equally tiring, as they are 
extremely slippery, and one may chance to meet now 
and then with a troublesome fall if in too great a 
hurry. The pedestrian therefore is obliged to pick 
out his steps almost as fastidiously as though he were 
walking over a London crossing in a pair of patent 
leather shoes. In some of the streets an unbroken 
quiet seems to reign, whereas others are alive with 
the yells of itinerant vendors of fruit or milk, the 
latter article being carried about in skins, and squirted 
through a small orifice into the cup or jug of the pur- 
chaser; or else they are crowded with a noisy and 
idle population, sitting on benches outside the houses, 
chatting, smoking, and drinking coffee all day long, 
and blinking lazily at any European that may hap- 
pen to be passing. At the absence of the latter in 
these quarters I was perfectly astonished. The 
French rarely ever penetrate beyond their own part 
of the town, and I have walked for ten minutes 
through the most crowded purlieus of the Moorish 
town without coming across a single Christian. A 
general idea, too, seems to have gained ground that 
the native population invariably speak the language 
of their conquerors. So far, however, from such being 
the case, I can confidently state that those who do so 
are quite the exceptions, and consist principally of 
men who are in official, or other situations, in which

daily communication with tha French is necessary. 
In asking,my way to the different localities in the 
old town it was rarely that 7 could light on a native , 
whp undgrstood aught but “Arabic, a shake of the 
head, accompanied by the barbarised Sp&nish phrase 
“no sabe,” being generally the answer to aught that 
is not in their own tongue. Of the different localities 
of the streets, however near at hand, to which their 
conquerors have affixed European names, they seem 
totally ignorant, and®*not unfrequently after demand- 
ing from several shopkeepers in the same thoroughfare 
the situation of some particular region, and receiving 
from each an unqualified negation as to their acquain- 
tence with it, I have found that the next turning 
would most probably indicate the solution to my 
query. Being once desirous of purchasing some 
Moorish slippers of four men who were working 
together in an aborigifial shop, I was obliged to wait 
at least five minutes, whilk they sent round for an 
interpreter who understood French, before I was able 
to conclude a bargain with them. 

The Moorish houses have no attempts at ornament 
or decoration as regards the exterior, and presend 
little to the passer by save bare white walls, and 
occasionally a window, about a foot square, grated 
with "thick crossed iron bars, through the interstices 
of which the smallest hand could scarcely be thrust. 
In the lower portion of the buttress there is generally 
a loop hole, through which the inmates can obtain 
a view of any one who might tap at the door, and 
determine whether their answer to the “ash-kool” 
(who is ¢here?) of the cautious negress within, is 
true or false. “It is needless to comment on the neces-

These steps are as irrationally large as the doors are 
small, being often half a yard each in height, and 
seem as though they were built for the stride of a 
giant,—an idea totally at variance with the poky 
little entrances above them.

of which a cooling jet of water, cast up from an 
ornamental fountain, glitters like myriad diamonds

The French, careful lest in 1 any out-of-the-way part 
of the town their presence ‘should be for a moment 
forgotten, have taken care to name most ef the streets 
either after their illustrious men, or the scenes of their 
African triumphs. Thus we have the Place Napoleon, 
Rue Kleber, Rue Rovigo, Rue Sidi-Ferruch, Rue de 
Chartres, Place Bresson, and several others. On 
passing a dingy littl® street at the upper part of the 
town I observed, to my surprise, Rue Sidney Smith 
written in Gallic capitals on one of the white walls. 
It was a narrow, dirty, street, and the only English 
name in Algiers might, I think, have adorned a 
better. I daresay the inhabitants often wonder where 
on earth the French could have picked up so un- 
pronounceable and barbaric an appellation as that 
comprised in the ters ‘five letters whose “th” they 
endeavor in vain to master? Among the conquerors 
it is of course “Rue Seednay Smeetz,” for who ever 
heard a Frenchman enunciate with correctness the 
surname of the Anglo Saxon descendants of Vulcan? 

The old fortifications, whose sombre walls have 
resisted so many aggressions both of the Christian 
and Infidel, still remain. They are of immense 
thickness, and run from the base to the summit of 
the hill, forming two sides of an isosceles triangle, 
between which the city is, of course, enclosed. Their 
construction is of the ancient Saracenic order, having

There “are several bazaars in the old town, which’ 
present a glittering assortment_of Oriental wares to 
the passer-by. The largest of these is about the same 
length as the Burlington arcade, did on each side 
are Arabs, Kuruglis, and Jews, sitting crdss-legged* 
in their respective. shops, with their merchandize 
hanging above their heads or strewn in front of 
them. Costly merchandize it is too, in most cases. 
There are burnouses from Tunis, Morocco slippers, 
Moorish vestments superbly embroidered with gold 
thread, Narghilés, Algerine bracelets, gigantic pipe 
sticks, gilt ostrich shells, and a host of other nic-nacs, 
for the acquisition of which, however, a well-filled 
purse is required. Ong may be dazzled with the pro- 
fusion of gilding around, but at the_same time a 
feeling of disgust at the Barbaric want of taste which 
invests everything with so tinsel-like an appearance, 
in spite of its magnificence is sure to predominate. 
The shopkeepers sit gravely within their shops, 
smoking long pipes, or sipping coffee, in apparently 
disdainful indifference to customers, but the moment 
they perceive the unmistakeable gait of an English- 
man the pipes are laid instantly aside, and new life 
seems to infuse itself into those passionless features. 
Then ensues a scene of bawling and shouting which 
would not be discreditable to the nightly roar of 
Billingsgate,—each man extolling, of course, his own 
wares, and crying down those of his neighhour. If 
he can but fig your attention for a moment he wilt 
thrust the whole contents of his shop into your face, 


54 THE CORSAIR 


taking care, however, to ask a pretty high price for 
anything that seems to take your fancy. Should you 
intend to purchase it you may offer him about half 
the sum he demands, which he will of cotitse refuse 
with an injured look, assuring you that he is letting 
you have it at cost price and consequently at a loss 
to himself. You move away towards another shop, 
and he instantly lowers his terms till they approach 
nearer and nearer to your bid, while you pretend to 
examine the goods of his neighbour who is in turn 
all anxiety to please you. With a face perfectly 
frantic, and at the same time a pathetic appeal to 
Allah, he thrusts the article into your hands, begging 
you to take it at once, at your own terms, ere he re- 
pents of the bargain. In this way you can bid the 
merchants one against another and thus obtain arti- 
cles at a reasonable rate, as they will do anything to 
secure your custom, conscious that if they only obtain 
one third of the original price they have still gained 
by the transaction. In their eyes—and in fact 
throughout the whole of Algiers—every Englishman 
is a living gold mine which they are determined to 
work to the utmost while they have the power, while 
his word is considered safe as a bond,—and not only 
the natives, but the French shopkeepers also, will 
trust him with any article however costly from their 
shops, relying on his national honor either to bring 
it back, or purchase it, at option. Hence it behoves 
Englishmen to be most scrupulous and exact in their 
dealings, not only for their own sakes, but for the 
sake of those of their countrymen who mpy follow 
in their footsteps. During the reignjof the Deys, 
the merchants who arrived with their caravans used 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 55 


to find accommodation for theraselves as well as their 
wares at ‘hese bazaars, which thus answered the ad- 
ditional purposes of an inn. , Honest trade, however, , 
rarely throve at Algiers in those good old times, when 
to make the slightest shew of wealth wés equivalent 
to signing your own death-warrant, and receiving the 
present of a bowstring from the generous hands of 
royalty itself, with an affectionate request that you 
would transfer yourself into Paradise before twelve 
o’clock. Besides, thé idea of obtaining goods by legal 
purchase was much too milksop and insipid for the 
rollicking pirates, and the visits of the foreign traders 
were consequently few and far between. Negro flesh 
from Timbuctoo generally found purchasers, but at 
last there was a glut even of that in the market, and 
we read of forty slaves being at one period usually 
given in exchange for a horse! Bigoted as they are, I 
believe the shopkeepérs would be sorry to return to 
the despotism of their former rulers, though they 
profess, at the same time, the most undying hatred 
for the French. There is a old prophecy rife among, 
them, delivered by a Marabout who flourished in the 
last century, and which I do not think it will be ir- 
relevant to mention here. It was to the effect that 
Algiers should be first stormed by the English, then 
taken by the French, and then retaken by the En- 
glish, who were to occupy it permanently. The first 
two of these events have already been realised in the 
expedition of Lord Exmouth and the battle of Sidi- 
Fer-uch, and many of the native population are 
eagerly expecting the conclusion of the prophecy, to 
which tiey look forward with singular pleasure. Nor 
is this superstition confined wholly to the Indigénes, 


56 THE CORSAIR 


for a French gentleman actually sold his house a 
short time ago, and returned to France, s@ confident 
was he of our advent in, Mauritania! Of course no 
importance could possibly be attached to a.prophecy 
of this kind, which after all is only oral, and was 
most probably invented by the ever superstitious 
Algerines, after their subjection, as a sort of pole-star 
of Hope which might constantly cheer them with its 
beams. In spite of their protestations, however, their 
hatred is in reality directed against Christians, inde- 
pendent of their nationality, and one government 
would doubtless be as galling to them as another. 
It is against common sense to suppose that Mussul- 
men could ever entertain the slightest real respect for 
people whom their most sacred book teaches them to 
regard as stubborn infidels, breathing forth unclean- 
ness and pollution from every pore. Under such 
circumstances, what shape could the genius of order 
assume save that of the gaping cannon or the flashing 
bayonet? Perhaps if she had ever assumed the same 
bristling and determined appearance in India we 
might not have learnt so terrible though so useful a 
lesson.

transferred the “vile metal” into the palm of the 
Israelite, who passed a high eulogium on his probity.

an occurrence, the Zouave coolly asked for the watch 
Which he alleged to have given in pawn for the 
money. The Jew, wrapt in ecstacy most probably 
at the idea of finding one righteous man in this dis- 
honest world, appeared not to heed the last demand, 
and turned slowly on his heel. But the Zouave 
repeated it with rather a menacing gesture, and the 
crowd, too, joined in the debate. The descendant 
of the Patriarchs changed color, and stammered most 
dolefully--a defect which is no joke in Arabic—at-

The Moorish coffee-houses—mere square white- 
washed rooms of different sizes, the fourth side of 
which opens into the street,—form an important 
feature in the town. Upon the walls are generally a- 
few pictures of wonderful green animals, into whose 
bodies letters and sentences from the Koran are in- 
corporated, while low divans covered with matting 
run round the apartment, upon which throughout 
the brightest and hottest day you will see crowds of 
Arabs sipping black coffee, smoking ‘long pipes, or 
else fast asleep. .They sit, of course, cross-legged, 
their black slippers being placed on the ground beside 
theia, and little conversation goes on, either because 
they are too indolent, or too deep in the enjoyment 
of the tobacco or “ hasheesh” which escapés ever and 
anon in white clouds from under thein moustachios, 


60 THE CORSAIR 


or through their distended nostrils. The cooking 
apparatus—consisting of a charcoal fire aad a few 
saucepans—is in the corner of the room, and the 
odour that issues therefrom is most delicivus. For 
the small sum of a sou a cup of this beverage is’ 
handed to you, accompanied by a pipe of tobacco, or 
a cigaret, should your fancy lead you to prefer it. 
You allow it to settle for a moment, (as a quarter of 
the cup, at least, is filled with the grounds) and then 
it is most refreshing and invigorating, partaking 
greatly when sweetened of the character of a liqueur. 
For the benefit of those amateurs who may wish to 
try their hands at this luxury I hereby give the true 
recipe, obtained vird voce from an Arab, for its fabri- 
cation. 3 

Take the best coffee you can obtain, and grind it 
yourself, to be sure there is no chicory or chicanery 
in it. Fill about a quarter of a small cup with it, 
and upon that pour boiling water, adding little more 
than a tea-spoonful of sugar to the mixture. Then 

-take the smallest covered tin pot you can procure, 
and empty the whole contents of the cup therein. 
nace it upon a charcoal fire for three or four minutes, 
and then pour it back into the cup. Give it another 
monient to settle, and if the compounder does not 
find it excellent the fault must be laid on the shoul- 
ders vf the Arab who gave the recipe, not on those 
of the author who publishes it. 

Curiously enough the trade of barber is generally 
amalgamated with that of coffee-house keeper, and 
the divans are occupied nearly the whole morning by 
Arabs and Moors who are undergoing the process of 
a “shave.” dt is more picturesque, perhaps, than 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 61 


pleasant to find oneself endeavoring to enjoy a cup of 
coffee between two swarthy men, their faces covered 
with soap-suds, and the disty lather dropping down 
at your feet with each sweep of the razor. It is still 
more remarkable, however, to see whole rows of little 
boy. 8, from the age of ten downwards, gravely await- 
ing their turn for the steel, and offering themselves 
to the creaming brush with the sang- -froid of bearded 
men. This fact may perhaps appear startling to 
Jones, who during his tenderer years has often shaved 
himself with the shoe-horn, but would have run away 
at the very sight of the paternal dressing case. To 
ease Jones’s mind let us inform him that these little 
men, however ambitious of bristles, yield up, not their 
faces, but their heads, to the barber, who sweeps off 
every morsel of hair, with the exception of a tuft on 
the pericranium called the “Mohammed.” From 
boyhood to manhood this tuft is nover removed, for 
by its aid their religion teaches them that the Pro- 
phet will lift them up into paradise. 

During the evening these coffee-houses are en-* 
livened with strains of melody. Now according to 
our limited and terrestrial notions we understand by 
“music” something which is, at all events, not dis- 
tasteful to the ear. The vocal performances of: the 
Arabs, however, bear a most suspicious resemblance 
to that famous tune, during the performance of which 
society deplored the loss of a too susceptible feline 
member. I think one night’s experience, dotted 
down as I found it, will shew ample reason why I 
did nof, frequent these temples of the unmusical 
oriental muge. On a raised divan in a corner of the 
coffee-house sat three Arabs, one of wi.om handled a 


62 THE CORSAIR 


small instrument like a Chinese guitar, with two 
strings of catgut, from which of course a véry feeble 
sound could be produced, while another drew a bad- 
ly-resined bow across a violin which he held in the 
manner of a violoncello, and the third played on a 
peculiar drum of red pottery, called “rebeb,” which 
resembled one of the modern water-coolers with a 
parchment bottom to it, and gave forth a most as- 
tonishing volume of sound for its size. I have rarely 
distinguished more than two or three different varie- 
ties of Arab chants, and these merely consist in the 
descent of a trilling gamut of five or six: notes, each 
note being exchanged indiscriminately, and without 
the slightest regard to effect, for its sharp or flat, as 
the taste of the performer may suggest, the singers 
invariably straining their voices into a harsh falsetto, 
and emitting the sounds through the nose in the 
manner of the melancholy Chinese who howl now 
and then about the streots of London. Of course 
there is never the slightest attempt at harmony, (of 
‘which they seem not to have the remotest idea) and 
which at Algiers certainly sleeps undisturbed beneath 
the heap of “jarring atoms.’’* Perhaps these remarks 
may afford a feeble idea of the performances of the 
musical trio alluded to above. The man with the 
drum sang lustily, and endeavoured with his voice 
and instrument to outstrip the other performers. He 
had thick and bushy eyebrows, which he raised and 
depressed alternately according to the high or low

superior to these barbarous yellings, else we must 
have a very mean opinion of his highness if he 
allowed himself to be roused by them to madness, 
or melted into amorous phases of “spooneyism.”

you might easily take it, in the distance, for a mosque.

has been there all his life is the “couadji” or pro-

in that lonely place is & question by no means easy 
of solution. The divan is very low and dirty, and 
the roof is festooned with centenary cobwebs woven 
by the large African spiders, which have the appear- 
ance of dirty towels hanging from the ceiling. In 
spite of these “little inconveniences,” however, I 
was furnished with a cup of most excellent coffee, 
which greatly invigorated me after a hot walk. There 
is another coffee-house, too, by the “Point Pescade,” 
which is equally ancient, and outside which a small 
trellised arbor, covered with the leaves of a trained vine, 
forms a most agreeable shade from the sun. Many a 
time have I ensconced myself therein, thinking over 
my own thoughts, to which the smiling landscape 
could hardly fail in giving aught but a pleasant turn.

This “Point Pescade” is about three miles to the

cactus-bordered road runs that leads to it, is broken . 
ever and‘anon by numerous creeks and crevices, in

until the convenient moment for pouncing on the 
unconscious victim-ship. The point itself is a rough 
steep neck of rock, stretching into thessea, and upon 
the end of it is a ruined fort supposed to be built by 
Barbarossa, while another fortress stands more in- 
land, erected about one hundred years ago by a 
pirate of the name of Abdy. These defences must 
have been perfectly impregnable from the sea in 
consequence of the height and steepness of the rock, 
down which I vainly attempted a descent. The walls 
are of small stones joined by a powerful cement, and 
of amazing strength and thickness, while the cannon, 
old and rusty, are still peeping from their respective 
embrasures with the stands actually decaying be- 
neath them. On the breech of the widest was sleeping 
a cat, her body lazily coiled up over the touch-hole. - 
Here was a tableau-virant of Peace, which wanted 
but the pencil of a Landseer to perpetuate the con- 
ception to admiring ages. Years past, and the iron 
Voices of those guns bellowed over the echoing ceru- 
lean of the Mediterranean. Tow faithful in its

The Arab coffee-house keeper, however, is not 
without his share of pride, and expects you to be 
perfectly satisfied with whatever he vouchsafes to 
afford you.. If you turn up your nose at the dirt 
around he is perfectly ready to turn you out bodily, 
preferring your room to your company, however 
well paid. A French lady, the wife of an officer, 
walking one day with her husband in the country 
came upon one of these coffee-houses, and expressed 
her audible disgust at its unseemly appearance in 
her own tongue, little deeming that the Arab host, 
who was standing outside, could understand what 
she said. He was sharp enough, however, to arrive 
at her meaning, and placing himself in the door-way 
very coolly refused to admit the officer, who was about 
to seek refreshment. And neither persuasion nor 
threat could influence the Arab to alter his resolution 
of not moving a single inch to allow the fastidious 
Gaul entrance into the homely precincts of his 
coffee-house. 

Like the coffee-houses, the Aboriginal shops are 
mere square apartments opening into the street, and 
with not the slightest pretence to a window, the 
wares being sheltered generally by a wooden pro- 
jection above, after the manner of the marts at Con- 
stantinople. Fruit shops and boot makers abound 
in the greatest quantity, but besides these thero are 
sherbet shops, gold lace shops, turners, blacksmiths, 
and tailors,—the largest being rarely more than four 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 67 


feet square, and having, at the most, three or four 
men within. The floor is covered with matting upon 
which the workmen squat ¢ross-legged, with their - 
shoes before them, working incessantly the live-long 
day, though but few purchasers seem’ to present 
themselves. So contracted are the dimensions of the 
shops in some instances that there is barely room for 
one man within, and I was extrentely struck ono 
day with a burlesque fruiterer’s, which was scarcely 
higher than three feet, and certainly not wider than 
two, and where the pile of fruit in front, reaching 
almost to the top, left just room for the head of the 
occupant to be seen above it, like that of a Jack-in- 
the-green. Of no greater area than the above was a 
blacksmith’s, where two men were bending with their 
faces in such close proximity to the anvil that the 
myriad sparks poured over them like a fiery shower 
bath ; and how they manage to escape the burns and 
scorches with which their daily work must threaten 
them is a mystery I cannot fathom. The most 
curious shops, however, are the turners, where you 
see Arabs working cross-legged at diminutive lathes, 
holding the articles to be turned, such as rings cr 
beads, between their toes, while they work the ma- 
chine with the hands. The toe is with them as useful 
a part of the body as the fingers, and in that respect 
they follow the true dictates of Damé Nature, who 
doubtless never intended that toes should be such 
useless, trampled on anomalies, as they usually are 
with booted Europeans. Drop a small coin on the 
ground behind you, and an Arab, while staring you 
earnestly in fhe face, will pick it up with his foot 


68 THE CORSAIR 


and transfer it to his hand, before you have time to 
stoop and look for it. I believe too, on an emergency, 
he would even empty your pockets with the same 
dexterous member, if you would give him a fair 
chance. Happily that is a state of perfection to . 
which London pickpockets can never arrive in our 
present age of “confined understandings.”

* Al-Sirat is a bridge, fine as a hair, and sharp as #eabre, beneath 
which is the eternal fire. On the day of Judgment all men are supposed 
by Mussulmen to be compelled to pass over this bridge, thq virtuous 
being of course sustained by Mohammed himself, As for the wicked, 
they topple over at the first step, into the burning lake, Among the 
Persians, it is known as Poul-Serrha; and a Persian Mussuiman, when 
he wishes to use an offensive qxpression to his neighbour, remarks, “Just 
see if I don’t trip you up as you cross Poul-Serrha, if I happen to be 
behind you!”

Axruoucu I had acquired the usual amount of 
book information relative to the costume and ap-

that I was for the moment completely taken by sur- 
prise when the first real, live, daughter of the Pirate 
crossed my path. And I defy the best picture that 
ever was painted, or the best description that ever 
was penned, to give an adequt.te idea of the feelings, 
impossible to analize, which crowded unbidden around 
me, as I looked upon that strange, outlandish dress, 
enshrouding, as it did, # being whose thoughts, habits 
and position, were so totally and irreconcilably at 
yariance with those of her more enlightened European 
sisterhood. ; 

It was on first leaving my hotel and emerging 
from the Rue de la Marine that I encountered five 
figures, clad_,in white from tip to toe, and walking 
with so noiseless a step that they reminded me, more 
than anything I had ever seen, of our nursery ideas 
of a ghost. Tall and weird-looking they were. and 
stalked along with strides that would frighten any 
but a streng minded woman, discoursing at the same 
time in a tone which, as the lower par. of their faces

A few words concerning the uses uf the henna (or as it is pronounced 
in Arabic “Khennah”) may not be irrelevant here. The henna, when 
prepared for use, is of a greenish-yellow hue, and has a strong resem- 
blance to light-colored snuff. The odour that iesucs from it is, according 
to European tastes, rather fetid, although among the natives of Bar- 
bary it is regarded as an agreeable and refreshing bouquet. It is planted 
in great quantities in the neighbourhood of Tunis, from whence it is 
imported largely:to Algeria and Morocco. The method of extracting 
the dye from it is as follows. A small portion is placed in a cup, and 
mixed with water to the consistency of paste. A poultice of the mix- 
ture is then applied to the part to be dyed, and left thore for half at 
hour or upwards, according to the desired intensity of the color. On 
removing the poultice the skin will be found to have acquired a tingé 
greatly resembling that of coral, whicn it requires the frictign of weeks 
to remove, and when the nail ig stained the dye is perfectly ineffaceable, 
and ia only removed by the actual growth of thv nail itself. The

innumerable rings. Sometimes the back of the hand 
is dyed biack with the col or cohol, which at other 
times gives a dark tinge to the nails in lieu of- 
the henwa, and either, as may be imagined, looks 
ugly at first to the fastidious glance of the European. 
But custom softens the barbarity, and reconciles one

The Moorish lady, however, has little cause to 
regret the concealment of visage. With her eyes 
alone she can do almost as much as the European 
with her full length face. There is no resisting the 
magnetic influence with which those full black orbs

are languid, they fire up, by turns, and in fact do 


Moorish women it many instances stain not merely the nails but the 
whole hand, and very often the feet. This singular habit is not merely 
ornamental, but to a certain degree medicinal, the henna praducing a 
refreshing coolness by acting #3 an astringent on the palms of the hands 
and the soles of the feet, and in India is often prescribed as an embro- 
cation in cases of fever by the native physicians, In the golden days of 
piracy the hides and coats of domestic animals were frequently tinged 
with it, and Mungo Park relates that bis Moorish master Ali, to whose 
malevolent caprice he was for so long exposed, invariably rode on a milk 
white horse whose tail was dyed red. 

It is almost superftuous to refer to the antiquity of this favorite dye? 
the earliest records of post-deluvial history affording proofs of its ex- 
istence and known uses. Of equal antiquity is the practice of painting 
the eyelashes and eyebrows with col, an operation performed by the 
women of Algiers by placing a brash filled with the moistened pigment 
between their eyelids, so that the involuntary motion of the eye dis- 
tributes the stain equally around the edge. In the Egyptian catacombs 
a@ bodkin was found enclosed in a small box filled with col extremely 
similar to those which are at present in use. Among the Babylonians, 
Hindoos, and Romans, it was in vogue, as well as among the Jews.

and there seems no reason why our translators should have been satisfied 
with the really incorrect expression “painted her face.” In the earlier 
ages this custom was not wholly confined to the,softer sex, and many of 
the Assyrian nobles, and even of the Mees and Romans, are represented 
as practising it. It is related by Atheneus that when Arbaces was 
introduced into the presence of Sardanapalus, by the influence of a 
household eunuch, he found the monarch carding purple among his 
ladies in a woman’s dress, his face, eyes, and eyebrows, being painted 
after the feminine fashion. His beard was shorn, and his face rubbed 
smooth with pumice stone, and when he had once looked on Arbaces he 
eretouched his eyes with the paint-brush—a sight that filled the Mede 
with indignant contempt. Of a less pleasant kind, however, was the 
painting which Parsondes, a young Assyrian noble, received at the 
hands of Nanarus, viceroy of Babylon, whom he had ridiculed for 
painting his eyes with col, and his face with white lead. According to 
this story (quoted by Fullom in his “ History of Woman,” from the 
pen of Nicolaus of Damascus) Parsondes having fallen into the power 
of the viceroy, the latter determined to revenge himself by rendering 
his victim utterly effeminate, and with this view commanded his 
myrmidons to shave and rub him with pumice stone, to anoint and 
bathe him twice a day, to underline his eyebrows and eyes, to plait his 
hair, to force him to sing and play on the harp, and to confine him 
entirely to-lfttle arts and accomplishments of the kind, “ill at length 
the unfortunate hero could hardly be distinguished from one of the 
softer sex.

have been exhibited in England in the same category

-the Moors there seems to be a perfect passion for this

In the generality of cases the hair seems to be 
dyed with henna, and we can only account for this 
singular custom by supposing that it took its origin 
from a necessity, the beard and locks being formerly 
colored to conceal the ravages of age. Musa, the 
conqueror of Spain, (according to Gibbon) “affected 
to disguise his age by coloring with a red powder 
the whiteness of his beard,” ond the ever imitative 
ladies struck with .the brilliancy of the dye may 
have adopted it, not merely as an ornament for their 
own dark-haired offspring, but also in order that 
by the habit becoming general the aged might not 
always be distinguished by the ruddy glow of their 
locks. 

It is rarely that Moorish ladies of rank ever leave 
the precincts of their own habitations. Whenever 
they do so, it is generally for the purpose of attending 
the bath, and their transit thither from their abodes 
is effected in a closely covered carriage, their faces at 
the same time being studiously concealed from the 
vulgar gaze. There was one respectable old lady 
who had been seventy years in Algiers, and being

The middling and lower classes of females are 
much more free and unrestrained in their actions 
than their richer sisters. They walk about like 
Europeans in the public thoroughfares, make pur- 
chases in the French shops, regardless in some cases 
whether it be a man or woman who serves them, and 
do not object now and then to stand on the outskirts 
of a crowd when anything out of the common is 
going on. Many of them speak French very fairly, 
having acquired it from their intercourse with the 
civilized world, and some of the younger ones are 
even able to read and write it as well as their native 
Arabic. For the latter important change in the men- 
tal condition of the rising generation the Algerines 
are entirely indebted to Madame Luce, a lady whose 
name, in connection with reform, ought to be as im- 
mortal in French history as that of Sir Robert Peel 
in our own annals. Many of them work and em- 
broider extremely well, and by their cleverness of 
fiuger can make a very tolerable living. The ostrich 
feather fans are principally the work of their hands, 
and are in some cases a source of great profit to them. 
In these respects they are a thousand times better off 
than hundreds of our own poor milliners,’ whose 
strength ebbs daily away at the needle’s point, and 
whose slender remuneration is barely sufficient to 
drive the fieud Starvation from the gloomy perspec- 
tive. Civilization tramps forward with resistless feet 
of iron and places upon the shoulders of our myriad 
white slaves the padded ladder upon which climbs 
Fashion with her rustling silks. There is,but one 
dread meaos—the pawnshop of Beauty—for the poor 
forlorn drudge to escape from its crushing weight

first of her nation that conceived the grand idea of 
educating the Moorish girls. For this purpose she 
Jeft Paris on her own resources and set up a school at 
Algiers. Her difficulties in obtaining even a single 
pupil were almost insurmountable, and nothing but 
her decision of character could have overcome them. 
Without a single exception, the Moorish women 
refused to entrust their children to the dangerous 
precepts of an infidel, but at length the proffered 
reward of two francs a week for every child sent had 
the effect of inducing some of the poorer parents to 
alter their resolution, and a few girls attended under 
the stringent promise that they were to be instructed 
in no principles or ideas contrary to the precepts of 
the Koran. Poor Madame Luce was pained by these 
conditions but resolved to accept them, trusting that 
she might by indirect hints open their eyes to the 
gleam of Truth. She made herself perfect mistress 
of Arabic, and taught her pupils to read and write 
in that language, as well as French, giving them at 
the same time as good an education in other things 
as falls to the lot of many a respectable European 
child. Her funds being at last exhausted she applied 
to the Government, who granted her the sum of ten 
thousand francs (four hundred pounds), and having 
at last convinced the Moorish women of the utility 
of education her school soon increased in size, and 
instead of paying she was herself paid at length for 
her laborious efforts. Scarce had her academy in- 
creased its proportions ere the friend of a creature in 
office obtained a monopoly for the same rurpose, 
settled in Algiers, soon filled her own benches, and

Personally, Madame Luce has a fine open face,

One little Mauresque there was who formed a strange

They discard the yashmak, however, in the streets, 
and have no particular walking costume. Strange 
and rare sight was it, even in the present age of 
miracles, to witness these little Moorish children 
shackled by the fetters of Islamism and yet emerging 
from the seclusion allotted to their sex by past ages 
to attain the book-learning so lately confined to the 
polished dames of Europe, and conferred on them at 
the same time by one whom they are taught to regard

to watch their nimble fingers as they embroidered

But still stranger was the anomaly as they sat side

Under the French rule the Jews with their usual 
money-making genius are a, thriving race, enjoying 
full privileges and immunities in common with French 
subjects. A magnificent synagogue has been erected 
for them by the conquerors, fn addition to many 
others which they have built with their own hands. 
Their dress is of the oriental cut, consisting of turban, 
jacket and bagged trowsers. By many of the younger 
Jews jackets of a bright blue color are worn, with a 
white furban and black trowsers; while the elder 
Jews, as before stated, adhere to the rigid black by 
which they were originally compelled to distinguish 
themselves. Their cast of cofintenance is decidedly 
handsomer than that of their European brethren, and 
their eyes and eyebrows are darker and more piercing. 
Still I have seen many an old Jew striding along in 
his sombre dress, who, with his sinister look, white 
hair, bushy eyebrows, and wrinkled hand clenching 
convulsively the knotted staff, seemed the very per- 
sonification of a Shylock—ready to take your heart’s 
blood should you fail in the payment of your bond. 
There is a geeat absence among them of the hooked 
nose—an adornment of nature so conspicuous among 
the Israelites of the Minories—and they are of rather 
taller stature than their brethren across the water.

106 The corsaIR 


their own bread, and the original way in hich this 
operation is performed by the Algerines, whether 
* Israelites or Mohammedans, deserves no§ce. The 
wheat is put in a conical trough with a hole at its 
base from which the grains run between two gigantic 
millstones, of which the uppermost rotates slowly on 
a moveable axis .by the agency of two beams of im- 
mense length fixed therein. These beams are tuned 
by a horse or mule, the bridle projecting from whose 
mouth is fastened to one beam, while a rope passed 
round its middle is secured to the other. It is by the 
latter beam that motion is communicated to the stone, 
the former acting merely as a guide—always seeming 
to pull the animal on while its fellow-radius is in 
motion. Round the neck of the horse a bell is fixed, 
so that watching is not always necessary, as the ces- 
sation of the tinkle is sufficient proof of the cessation 
of motion. And.the wheat” ground between these 
millstones falls into a proper receptacle beneath. 

It must argue well for the digestion of the Abori-

tightly on their backs, uttering at the same time the 
most heartrending cries it is possible to conceive. 
After the camel haf risen one of its fore legs is 
doubled and strapped beneath it, so that it rests on 
the other three, and this being done they limp away 
and join the drove, their legs being unfastened when 
all is ready to start; then off they go—and I have 
seen them in the open country trotting away at such 
a rate with an Arab on their back that it was a won- 
der how the rider could keep his seat.*

The Moors still keep up the practice of polygamy, 
though at the same time I believe there are many of 
them who are content with the society of one wife.. 
Wives with them, it is needless to observe, are not 
the soft and tender helpmates, the shofers of their 
joys and griefs, and the soothers of their anxious 
hours, as our English understanding of the term 
teaches us. There are no doubts or distractions on 
the part of the gentlemen, and no coyness or coquetry 
with the lady previdus to the auspicious event, the 
idea of courtship, from the secluded, life of the women, 
being of course entirely out of the question. The 
French “marriage de convenance” is the term whose 
signification approaches nearest to them,—marriage 
being a mere commercial transaction which the gen- 
tleman takes in a very cool sort of manner after he 
has paid down in hard cash the sum demanded for 
the invisible “ bundle Of charms.” Hamet the shoe- 
maker haying amassed a few extra napoleons by 
many hard years of cobbling feels melancholy one 
morning on discovering that the coins which he has 
so carefully hoarded in his cellar are beginning to 
tarnish. In this dilemma he meets his neighbour 
Akhbar the sherbet-seller, and the following conver-

And the young lady is given over veiled to the

countenance of her future lord and master.” 

Whether the newly-married couple be poor or rich 
it would be sadly in opposition to the customs of 
every nation on the globe if there were not a little 
rejoicing and merry-making on the occasion. Among 
the Algerines ladies innumerable are invited to the 
celebration of the nuptials, but not the shadow of a 
single gentleman save that of the bridegroom obtrudes 
itself on the feminine assembly. From those who 
have attended on these occasions I am informed that 
it seems to be almost as slow an affair as an old 
maid’s tea party, everyone being most pdinfully

The bride was an exceedingly beautiful Princess, 
about eighteen years of age, and was attended by 
other Princesses, who acted as ladies-in-waiting and 
tiring women. She sat on a raised dais, and appeared 
more like a lovely statue than a breathing creature of 
flesh and blood, as it was almost impossible to detect 
a single movement in the position of her graceful 
limbs. The guests occupied couches placed round 
the spacious apartments, and were constantly regaled 
with cakes and other confectionaries, handed to them 
by the attentive domestics. Mussulman etiquette 
requires that the bride shall neither speak, smile, or 
even ‘look at any of her friends on the day of her 
marriage and in strict accordance with this custom 
the lady fixed her eyes on the ground as though she 
were dazed By an irresistible magnets influence,

were painted with col, her eyes were touched with 
belladonna, which rather detracted from their soft 
beauty by expanding the pupil and giving them an 
unnaturally brilliant appearance; and little gold stars 
were stuck over different parts of her face. Her nails 
were of course tinged with the deepest dye of henna, 
and her hands were stained black on the palm as well 
as the outside,—the dingy col being generally used 
in this manner on all festive occasions. The dressing 
lasted nearly three hours, and was a most tedious 
operation both for the actors and the assembled 
guests, a band of negresses being in attendance 
with fifes and tomtoms who played a particular tune 
for each portion of the dress as it was adjusted, no 
new part being put on till the sable musicians had 
changed the character of the melody. Some of .the 
negresses danced and others sung, and after every 
two or three steps they came round for money, which 
was given to them by the bride’s relatiors from a 
small golden box, the lid of which was studded with 
diamonds. Then, at a given signal tiie ladies, who

which even my intelligent informant would have 
found difficult to decide.
