CHAPTER VY. 


Tue Mosques in Algiers are numerous, and some 
are fine specimens of Moorish architecture, but in the 
time of the Deys their number was treble what it is 
at present, so many of them having been either pulled 
down or converted into buildings for secular purposes. 
Of the latter kind the finest is that attached to the 
Casbah, or late palace of the reigning despots, and its 
mutilation must be sadly deplored by the true lover 
of the beautiful, its light marble pillars, even, and 
delicately carved entablatures being almost entirely 
hidden behind piles of coats, shakos, or other mili- 
tary paraphernalia. The scimetar of the Infidel has 
yielded indeed to the Christian sword, but it must be 
painfully evident that the minds of the captors are 
even more Infidel in matters of decorative art than 
those of the race who have left behind, for the won- 
der of succeeding generations, the matchless colonnades 
of the uncrumbling Alhambra. 

The two largest Mosques are those situated in the 
Place du Gouvernment, and the Rue de la Marine. 
The former owes its peculiar shape to a F-1ropean 
slave, an architect, who, on being commanded by the 
Dey to undc.take its construction, toox care that it 


e 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. _ lz 


should be built in the form of a cross. The grand 
mistake was not discovered till it was completed, and 
the circumstance was of course related to the Dey,, 
who was so incensed at the audacity of the “Christian 
dog” that he commanded his head to be instantly 
struck off. The Mosque is surmounted by two cupolas 
and a minaret, and is of a dazzling white, the minaret 
being ornamented at the top by porcelain tiles of fan- 
tastic pattern. The interior, however, far surpasses 
the exterior, the dorfies being superbly painted with 
ornamental sentences from the Koran, similar sen- 
tences being engraved over all the dgors. The Keblah, 
or sacred arch, which is built in the direction of Mecca, 
is surrounded with a carving in stone, delicate as lace 
work, and close to it is a door which leads on to a 
terrace, sacred as the niche itself, on which true 
believers take an airing and imagine that they are 
gazing towards the holy city. In the earlier ages of 
Mohammedanism the Keblah consisted of a visible 
point in the horizon, to which the eyes were to be 
directed in order that attention to prayer might be 
better kept up. Later however, it was found more 
convenient to introduce the arch, which, under the 
same appellation, answers of course a similar purpose, 
besides being more plainly perceptible by the worship- 
pers within the building. From the middle of the 
floor rises a railed platform from which ,the’ Koran 
is neighed forth (for no other term will express the 
unmusical drone which they call chanting) and a 
small, wooden gallery running along the right wall 
is reserved for women and Infidels upon gala nights. 
The floor is entirely covered with matting, but before 
the niche is gpread a carpet for the reader of the 


118 THE CORSAIR 


Koran, which Christian feet are not allowed to soil. 
By the principal entrance. is a fountain, with an im- 
mense basin, in which true believers wash their hands 
and feet’ without soap, the latter article being unne- 
cessary- to the faithful, who believe that Mohammed 
will clean their finger nails for them in’ Paradise. 
This is the wholé interior of a Mosque—and it is 
upon this pattern that all the Mosques in Algiers, 
and everywhere else I believe, are constructed. 
The Mosque in the Rue de la Marine (Djemaa 
Kebir or large Mosque) is much darker than the one 
alluded to above, and an additional fountain is placed 
outside, in the street. The Hotel-des Ambassadeurs, 
where I lodged, is just opposite the Mosque, and of 
this fact I was painfully convinced by being awoke 
now and then by the brazen lungs of the Muezzin, 
who mounts the minarets thrice in the day and twice 
at night to call the faithful to prayer. Let me, how- 
ever, do the Algerines the justice to say that the call 
is nobly responded to—and old and young, rich and 
poor, leave their beds by night, and théir occupations 
by day, at the invitation of those stentor tones, to 
prostrate themselves before that beneficent Being 
whom they worship under the name of Allah. What 
can the more enlightened European say, as those 
benighted creatures pass him daily, each striving to 
be first''in adoration? Attempts to convert them 
are against the spirit of the French law—nor do they 
seem to have the slightest wish to be initiated into a 
religion to which the conquerors themselves seem to 
give so little heed. A Sunday on which the usual 
traffic of the week is carried on without the'slightest 


AND His CONQUEROR. _ 119 


interruption—doctrines which the mass of the people 
cannot or do not attempt to understand—and churches 
where the real spirit of worship gives place to tinsel 
effect, are not the magnets which can allure the Mo- 
hammedan from the sensual Paradise premised as a 
reward to his constancy and fidelity.” © 

, Cleanliness, according to the precepts of the Koran, 
is the key of prayer, and many a Mussulman as he 
passes to his daily work will perform his ablutions at 
the fountain, even tlfugh he may not have time to 
enter the Mosque. Sometimes two or three of them 
meet, and stick their slippers in crevices of the wall 
while they wash first one foot and then another, di- 
vesting themselves lastly of their burnouses, and 
splashing their faces and arms all over. Then they 
dry themselves with the burnous, which thus forms a 
very convenient portable towel for hands, legs and 
face, after which theyepop it on again, as well as 
their slippers, parting from each other either as 
friends or foes according as the ablutions begin 
from the “funny bone” of the elbow or the extreme 
tip of the little finger. -And the little patches of wet 
on the burnous add greatly, as may be imagined, to 
the picturesqueness of their toute ensemble.

* No wonder that Mussulmen adhere so steadily to the precepts of 
Mohammed, when that imposter (or enthusiast) has given them such 
comfortable assurances respecting their future state. An the day of 
Judgment, the Koran declares that Jews, Christians and Infidels (the 
latter term being applied to all who have not embraced the doctrines of 
Islam) will be driven into the lowest hell, and condemned, without 
hearing, to an eternity of torture. For the Moslem, however great his 
offences, Paradise gleams ever in the distance, and at the utmost the 
fire of Al A. xf (or purgatory) will purify his soul, for spaces of from 
nine hundred to seven thousand years, according to the crimes commit- 
ted by him :a the flesh.

I need scarcely remark that a few years ago it was 
death to a Christian to enter a Mosque, and the whole 
, building had to be whitewashed and re-painted if he 
* but set foot in it, ® great was the defilement supposed. 
to emanate from his touch. Now, introition is not dif- 
ficult, save in one or two Mosques, to visit which a 
special order’is required. In every case, however, the 
shoes must be taken off, and the French themselves 
enforce this rule, it being as much a mark of respect, 
as removing the covering from the head in a Christian 
House of Prayer, besides having the prestige of the 
gteatest antiquity. No one, too, would wish to tram- 
ple on the scruples of a people, who whatever may 
be the errors of their credence yet believe what they. 
consider the essential truth, and the fervour of whose 
devotion it would be well if many a more enlightened 
congregation would imitate. The French, too, are 
bound to protect their worslip, as it was expressly 
mentioned in the ‘treaty of General Bourmont, pro- 
posed by himself “that their women and religion 
should be respected,”—conditions to which the last 
Dey was glad to subscribe, as he thus prevented the 
entire demolition of the Pirate city.

off our‘shoes. His, happening to be unfortunately 
new, were not disengaged in a moment from his feet,

them up: Thanks to the matting on the floor it was 
by no means a disagreeable process walking about

Keblah and stood within it, chatting lightly together 
and watching the devotees with the utmost sang-froid. 
T certainly thought the hot-blooded Algerines would

clenched their fists, and eyed them like tigers eyeing 
their prey. Several jumped up hastily and ran off, 
preferring rather to curtail their devotions than re- 
main in the Mosque while their most sacred place 
was being polluted. It certainly was the most 
execrable piece of bad taste I ever witnessed, and 
had it occurred in the earlier years of the conquest I 
believe they would have answered for the insult by 
their blood. We left the Mosque in a short time, not 
caring to be ranked in a category with such unworthy 
represcatatives of races that are called superior, re- 
flecting as we put on our shoes that acts like those 
are certainly not calculated to gain the good will of 
a fanatical people. : 

The first thing a Mussulman does on entering a 
Mosquc is to perform his ablutions. THen he ad- 
vances to within a reverend distance from the Keblah 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 123 


and holds up his hands like a person in astonishment 
at his own absurdities. Then he places his hands 
together, open at an acute angle before him, as though 
he were trying to catch rain water; which is their 
form of supplication instead of our more*expressive 
one of clasping the hands. And then he makes three 
low bows to the sacred arch, after which he prostrates 
himself at full length and touches the ground thrice 
with his forehead, his knees acting as a fulerum and 
his heels flying, as a ‘hatter of course, each time into 
the air. And then he takes his seat among the other 
worshippers, and attends to the reading of the Koran. 
Except on particular occasions there are no grand 
services, the greater portion of their worship seeming 
to be performed individually, or at most in parties 
of half-a-dozen, as if they had no conception of the 
efficacy of the united prayers of a large assemblage.* 

During the Ramadhan the Mosques are illuminated 
all over with small lamps, which has of course a most 
brilliant effect, and then the nightly services are 
characterized by the attendance of a numerous con- 
course which is more in accordance with our ideas 
of a congregation, all performing their devotions at 
the same time, and enunciating with unanimous 
voice the perfections of Allah. There is something 
thrilling in the worship of a multitude, though they 
be but idolaters—something that appeals te the sense 
rather than the ear in that passionate devotion to a 
superior Being, of whose existence all nations though 
sunk in the deepest ignorance have ever had an innate 
perception. Otherwise the effect might be attributable

to the blent tones of myriad human voices, the power 
of which, even though wild and inharmonious, can 
be surpassed by few instrumental combinations. Nay, 
so inexplicably sweet and inimitable is the music of 
vocal melody that it has been asserted by the most 
eminent musicians that the full power of the largest 
organs cannot drown the high soprano notes of a 
solitary female singer, provided only they be delivered 
with moderate strength and confidence. 

On the fourth of April conimenced the great fast 
of the Ramadhan—the anniversary of that month 
so sacred to Mohammedans, in which their prophet 
received the celestial proof-sheets at the hands of the 
angel Gabriel. The night itself on which the Koran 
was let down from heaven is termed Sailat-el-Khadir, 
or night of power, and is considered of greater value 
than a thousand nights put together. According to 
Mussulman credence nothing evil that is then com- 
mitted is looked on in the light of a crime, the very 
idea of wickedness being out of the question on so 
agreeable an evening. The value of good deeds is 
of course multiplied then to infinity, and prayers if 
offered up at the particular moment when the silken 
wlume was handed to Mohammed are instantly 
heard. Of course the prophet was not stupid enough 
to let his followers into the secret of the then Green- 
wich titne. so that a"pious Moslem, if he wishes to 
catch the opportunity, must repeat his petition from 
sunset to sunrise. A young negress {according to 
Long) was once fortunate enough to hit upon the 
right moment, and offered up an instant request for 
a good head of hair,—the Algerine phrase which 
expresses that desire is rendered “Iord wake my

e 


head large””—and her prayer was answered so literally 
that they had to knock down part-of the wall next 
morning fd let her out into the street! Every day 
in that month from three o’clock till sunset neither 
bit nor drop enters the mouth of the Mussulmen, 
who by the austerity of their self-mortification shew 
an endurance worthy of a better cause. Not even 
the commonest labourer will do so much as moisten 
his lips or smoke a, cigaret during the prohibited 
hours; and a merchant was even known to refuse to 
sell otto of roses in case the agreeable odour might 
please his senses of smell. The moment, however, 
that the boom of the evening gun echoes over the 
town they immediately set to work to make up for 
the deficiencies of the day. But little time is given 
them for this as their attendance dt the Mosques is 
required soon after, and no matter of what age or 
rank they rush in crowds to worship. Twice in the 
night they are awakened to eat; and this, coupled 
with the austere abstinence of the day, causes many 
of them to fall off terribly at the end of the month. 
Those who do not work may be seen lying about in 
coffee-houses, endeavoring to sleep away the hungry 
hours, their faces pale and wan even in slumber, 
while the busy little antelopes (?) emerging from 
the matting of the divans feast in pitiless myriads, 
utterly regarilless of the precepts of théir prophet, 
on the unconscious body of the unflinching votary 
of Islamism. i

AND HIS CONQUEROR. 127 


Attached to the Mosques are the courts of law, the 
administration of which is regulated principally by 
the Kora, some of whose sentences, executed in 
different styles of Arabic caligraphy, cover the walls. 
Entrance therein is refused to Christians; but they 
are allowed to peep in at the door on condition of 
strict silence. The Kadi sits at the upper end of the 
room, habited in a green turban and yellow slippers, 
with a whip in his hand; while around him the 
Khodjas or clerks take notes of the evidence. Women 
are not permitted within the precincts of the court, 
but have to wait in a separate chamber, and are 
obliged to give their evidence through a small grated 
window. Often have I amused myself by watching 
their covered heads bobbing energetically up and 
down behind the iron cross-bars, and a volubility of 
clack issuing from beneath those yashmaks such as 
Algerine women only°know how.to produce. The 
Kadi in the mean time sits perfectly motionless and 
preserves a dignified silence, never seeming to give 
heed for a moment to the feminine gutturals, though 
of course he takes them all in. He has power over 
minor offences, and can sentence to imprisonment 
and the bastinado, the latter being administered at 
the Bureau Arabe. The French wished to do away 
with this barbarous punishment, but were prevented 
by the unanimous voice of the Algerines; who were 
indignant at the idea of losing so important a national 
institution ! 

The Moslem writers seem earnest in dissuading 
people from soliciting at the hands of their sovereign 
the arduous and responsible post of Kadi, and even 


128 THE CORSAIR . 


mention with applause the names of these who 
suffered imprisonment rather than accept the obliga- 
tion. In fact many of the lawyers of old-insert that 
the acceptance of the office without compulsion is 
abominable, founding their opinion on the declaration 
of the prophet—“whoeyer is appointed Kadi suffers . 
the same torture as an animal whose throat is man- 
gled instead of being cut with a sharp knife.” Hence 
a Kadi is expected to be something far removed above 
human frailties, and his every action is a subject of 
cavil and discussion. He is, however, a person of 
wonderful importance among the Algerines, and 
when he enters the court his subordinates shew their 
sense of the dignity of his office by kissing him on 
the right shoulder. Can the origin of the expression 
“turning a cold shoulder to anyone” be traced to 
a, perversion of this oriental custom ? 

The outskirts of the town abound in Marobouts 
or Mosques raiged over the tombs of so-called saints. 
The finest is that of Sidi Abderrhaman el Tsdlebi, a

fever or noxious diseases. The entrance is through

Three rosaries of wooden beads hang above him to 
testify the number of pilgrimages he has performed 
to Mecca. These rosaries are by no means dissimilar

Our shoes, when we entered the Maratout, were

standing up in them for the first time. The rogue, 
too, instead of shewing signs of penitence was wicked 
enough to regret that he had not introduced a few 
earth-worms, which he said would have softened the 
effect of the sharper fragments!

shrine every Friday, and in the time of the Deys no 
Christian was allowed even to approach the ground 
rendered sacred by the remains of this idolized piece

It was by assuming the character and attributes of 
a Marabout that the great conjuror Robert Houdin 
managed to secure the submission of the rebellious 
chiefs to the French government, who had employed 
him on this singular mission. Every artifice in war, 
ag in love, is fair, and the idea of despatching a 
magician to bring about an object which neither fear 
nor love could effect has a touch of grandeur in it 
though it be but in its utter isolation from other 
instances. A book containing a full and copious ac- 
count of his dealings with the votaries of superstition 
has been pubjished, which ought to have given a great

incentive to the study of natural magic in France. 
During his short stay in the town Houdin amused 
himself often at the expense of the easily.mystified 
natives, and especially of the negresses. Concerning 
his dealings with them many rich anecdotes are 
afloat, of which the following is a specimen. 

Having gone up to a negress who was selling 
oranges, he demanded their price. “So many for a 
sou,” replied the sable exile of Timbuctoo. ‘That’s 
dear,” replied the conjuror, “however, let’s try one.” 
Having purchased half a dozen he cut one open, 
when lo! imbedded in the centre, as though it were 
coeval with the formation of the pips, lay a Napoleon! 
The negress as may be imagined exclaimed Bish- 
millah, and so did the crowd of native boys which 
had begun to collect. “That’s my Napoleon,” said 
Houdin pocketing it. Again he cut open an orange, 
and it is needless to say with the same result. The 
negress, though utterly mystified, was savage enough 
that she had parted with her oranges, and her feelings 
were by no means mollified as he caused the fruit to 
refund for the third time its apparently golden germ. 
Robert Houdin offered to purchase the basket-full, 
but of course the negress declined, positively: refusing 
to sell more, and neither the gens d’arme that the 
conjuror called up could force her to part with her 
apparentiy, hidden treasure. Of course Houdin 
feigned anger and the external passions: of disap- 
pointed avarice, but the negress was inexorable. 
Taking her basket into a street where she thought 
she might escape observation she seized a knife, and, 
commencing with the first, cut asunder every orange 
in her basket in greed of the shining, yellow. Of

course she spoilt her oranges, and found that they 
were really nothing removed from the common lot of 
vegetable,mortality: and here the anecdote comes to, 
an end. One can only hope that Robert Houdin 
reimbursed the poor woman afterwards fer his harm- 
less, though to her, expensive amusement. 

To return. Around the above-mentioned Mosque 
is a cemetery, covered with the graves of the highest 
families. The Moorish tombs are very peculiar, con- 
sisting of a small reétangle of ground ghout six feet 
long and a foot wide, enclosed by tiles. At the head 
of the tomb is a small stone rounded at the top, 
engraved. with the name of the deceased, and sur- 
mounted by the crescent. Close to this stone is a 
little vase of water which the relatives of the deceased 
keep constantly filled, as, according to the Moham- 
medan—or rather Algerine—credence, men rise again 
so many days after thoir burial, being lifted out of 
their tombs by the knot of hair at the back of their 
heads, and if they are thirsty they can thus take a 
drop of water by the way before leaving earth. Some 
of the tombs are large square buildings something 
like our family graves, upon which the women sit at 
regular intervals, and especially on Friday, which 
is the Mohammedan Sabbath, to mourn. By the 
sagacious prophet this day, considered in many parts 
of England as so invariably unlucky, was-cnosen not 
from any particular advantages which it offered, but 
merely for the sake of being distinguished from the 
Chrietian and Jewish days of rest. At a cemetery 
not far from the Jardin d’Essai we saw several women 
seated upon the tombstones, some of them apparently 
convulsed with grief. Within this cometery was

another small Marabout which we were about to

reverences, and the perspiration was literally pouring 
from his forehead with the vehemence of his emotions. 
It was an impressive sight, and we felt that it was 
wrong to disturb even by a careless footstep such real 
though misdirected piety, and with whispered thanks 
to our negro conductor we took our leave. 

On the sea-shore, below the Marabout of Sidi 
Abderrhaman, is a frowning fort, built partly into 
the sea, and connected with it is a prison, from the 
barred windows of which have risen many a tearful 
Christian prayer for liberty. It was built by the 
Spaniards, and taken from them by the ever unscru- 
pulous Algerines. Now, of all places in the world, 
it has been converted into barracks, and aS I walked 
through the melancholy stone arches beneath it I 
could not help pitying the soldiers who were kenneled 
therein. One poor fellow was lying in bed, weakened 
by one of the numerous fevers of Africa, while a 
thorough dreught was rushing through the cold arch

was buried alive for*his obstinacy. lis remains

The military parade ground extends along the sea 
shore and peasses over the spot where this cemetery 
formerly existed. * Here it is that the raw conscripts 
from France are drilled into shape, and here also the 
shining glaive of the guillotine does its fell work. 
When not occupied by the niilitary it is filled with 
Moorish boys amusing themselves with the innocent 
enjoyment of kite-flying. This pastime seems to 
be quite the rage among the’ tender children of 
the corsair, and for the number of kites, hexagonal, 
pentagonal, and quadrilateral, that you see fluttering 
above you in the breeze you might easily imagine 
yourself within the precincts of the Green Park on a 
summer’s day. Not merely is it an amusement for 
boys, but ‘srown up men often indulge in it, and it 
is wonderful to witness great sturdy Arabs rushing 
about, with the thermometer at 120°, and endeavor- 
ing to raise their colored aérial machines without the 
assistance of the slightest breath of air. Yet they do 
get them up in some mysterious way, with exertions 
that would glace a European hors de combat for a

week. And then, after fatiguing your eyes by

give up the attempt as hopeless, and for the next. 
few moments see everything in the most brilliant 
phantasmagorical shade of yellow and freen, with 
little phantom kites popping out of every nook and 
corner around. What can you do then but seat 
yourself on one of the warm rocks by the sea, and 
watch the magnificent scene till everything appears 
in its right color? The rippling Mediterranean is at 
your feet, the white sails of the numerous feluccas 
contrasting with its deep cerulean, while before you 
‘reclines the white city like a dainty maiden basking 
in the sunshine. And in the distance the bay 
sweeping so magnificently from Cape Matifoux, with 
the snowy peaks of the Jurjura rising in the back- 
ground, while above all is a sky of such wondrous 
coloring that the blud air seems perfectly tangible 
as it deepens every moment into greater intensity. 
And as you gaze into the fathomless azure vault the 
idea that must rise before your mind may be em- - 
bodied in the question—“who can conceive infinity 
when the wondrous yet limitable ether that we gaze 
on seems in itself £0 infinite ?” 

Fifteen years ago the parade ground, or place 
d’armes, was the scene of many an execution effected 
by the yataghan of the barbarian, death by the same 
instrument in the first few years of the French 
domination being awarded alike to all malefactors, 
Christian as well as Mussulman. But a Frenchman 
who wes sentenced to death for a murder having been 
brought os usual upon the scaffold the executioner, 
who by a désgraceful neglect on the part of the 


138 THE CORSAIR 


government was a Moor, trembled a little when he 
saw that it was the head of a Christian he was about 
to sever, and made two or three ineffectual blows 
before the death-stroke was inflicted. So enraged 
were the cblonists at the circumstance that a memo- 
rial was sent to Paris, responsive to which the more 
merciful guillotine replaced the crooked sword. But 
the steel of the latter has fallen upon many an un- 
fortunate Arab whose only crime was a refusal to 
submit to the conquerors, and a’ consequent resistance 
to their advancing bayonet. 

The Arab, noble to the last, rarely cringes before 
inevitable death. If he can escape it he will, or if 
he can resist it he will, but where he sees there is no 
escape and no resistance he meets his fate like a hero. 
A little time ago an Arab was sentenced to death for 
murder, and was brought to the place of execution 
in an omnibus. It was no vasy matter to get him 
out though, for like Barnadine he was unwilling to 
“get up and be hanged.” In the fierce wrath of his 
wild strength he kept at bay a whole posse of gens 
d’armes, who found it impossible to effect an entrance 
within the frail vehicle, till at last a noose was brought 
and one by one the giant limbs were seoured. Then, 
when he saw that all hope was vanished, he walked 
out quiet as a lamb, with deep resignation seated on 
his manly-features. With firm step he mounted the 
scaffold, and then for the last time gazed upon the 
waves that rippled before him in enviable freedom, 
and the white cupolas of his native city gleaming in 
the morning sun; then he stretched his hande-towards 
the city of the prophet and breathed forth his last 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 139 


prayer,—and the next moment the elements had 
claimed their own. 

At the top of the hill just above the Marabout of 
Sidi Abderthaman is a prison recently constructed 
by the French. The most peculiar feature in this 
building is the chapel, which has the appearance of 
an enormous bird-cage. The altar is raised on a 
high platform in the middle, and is approached by a 
side staircase, the whole being surmounted with tall 
strong bars of iron. The prisoners are brqught to 
the doors of their cells to hear mass, and then locked 
up again directly afterwards to meditate upon what 
they have heard. To the left of the building is the

On our route to the Maison Carrée we met nume-: 
rous parties of Arabs riding on camels, with baskets 
or sacks of vegetables piled up behind them. The 
camels were in’some instances going at a furious trot,

There is a dark side to every picture, and a cloud 
was cast over the usually cheerful English community 
by the death of one of its members—a litte girl over 
whose head but few summers had passed. The climate, 
which is very unfavorable to children, may have con- 
tributed its cota, I am afraid, to the untimely demise 
of the ill-fated innocent. Ere four and twenty hours 
have passed over the corpse it must be returned to its 
last cold home, according to the tenor of the French 
law—a proceeding, too, whicli the extreme heat of 
the climate renders imperative. It was so late before 
the preparations for the interment were completed 
that our little band of mourners were not able to 
leave the hotel in which the death had taken place 
till half-past six in the evening, and by the time we 
had arrived at the cemetery it was quite dark, being 
a very stormy night. The cemetery is in a very 
lonely situation, about a mile cut of the town, with the 
sea on one side and the mountains of the Boujareah 
on the other; and doubly gloomy did it appear under 
the sad circumstances. Nothing could exceed the 
solemnity of the scene, which, as the almost tangible | 
darkness thickened around, reminded one of some: 
terrible crisis in a dream, where the flesh creeps with 
expectation of a nameless approaching horror. We 
walked in procession behind the small coffin, covered 
with a wuite pall which the wind blew mockingly 
about as though eager to remind us that the lifeless 
clay within would soon become its spot. We closed 
round the small grave, and the fitful gusts of wind 
murmured mournfully among the distant mountains, 
increasing in violence till they wailed in a shrill note 
of despair’ over our bared heads: ++ was Nature 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 147 


weeping for the dss of a fair young flower. Earth 
to earth!—and as we-silently cast the dark mould 
over the offin forked lightning flashed vivid in the 
distance, like the awful harbinger of a world to come.” 
Dust to dust!—and as we threw the first fresh sod 
upon the once loving but now terribly heedless the 
sobs of the bereaved mother were heard even above 
the incessant roar of the foaming billows. All was 
at length finished, the clergyman from his unaided 
memory having repeated the funeral service; and sad 
and unbroken was the silence as we wended our way 
homewards, leaving atom to dissolve into atom, and 
element into element. Let them mourn! But the 
spiritual essence admitted to its bright eternity of joy 
unimaginable may be singing Hallelujahs in the 
presence of Eternal Perfection for a happy, happy 
release from the coarser trammels of its mortal prison- 
house. ;

CHAPTER VI. 


To evince surprise at any object met with in the 
course of travel is voted by the present generation 
decidedly unknowing, and to dilate on that surprise 
shockingly vulgar. The fact is that everybody has 
seen and done everything worth looking at in the four 
quarters of the globe, or at all events studied Murray 
to make up for their deficiencies as regards regions 
that may be beyond the reach,of their purse. Ennui 
and yawning are tue watchwords now; and English- 
men meeting casually in Abyssinia or Timbuctoo 
would as soon think of embracing a tiger os of 
speaking to each other without an introduction, no 
longer considering that mere brotherhood of race 
and language has more claim there to a civil greeting 
than it would have on the pavement of Piccadilly. 
“We made the acquaintance of the Joneses in 
Syria,” says the fair and languid denizen of a St. 
James’s Square drawing room, without of course 
bestowing a syllable upon the magnificent grove of 
palm-trees beneath the grateful shade of which the 
introduction probably took place. ‘ What a comfort 
it is being back in London,” exclaims the bati-room 
beauty, “I have only been at one eveniug party

Yet I think there are few tourists who can boast,

pirate. Ay, and it was one of the most ferocious of

The corsair vessels were not always the small tight 
craft we have been led to imagine from the wondrous 
tales of the sea which we took in so greedily during 
the green portion of our existence. Many of them 
were ships of thirty, forty, and fifty guns, with crews

a vessel they usually hailed her, and enquired of

The old pirate, like the Southern State planters of 
America, would lead one to believe that slavery in 
Algiers was a much lighter thing than it really 
seemed. From the accounts, however, that have 
come down to us from some of the unfortunate cap- 
tives the misery and ill-treatment they endured was 
so great that none but the most robust constitutions 
could endure it long. Independent of this, the 
terrible idea of working from sunrise to sunset in 
that almost tropical heat, the hours of leboumheing 
of course’ longer during summer than in the winter, 
would of itself be certain death to moxe Northerly

e 


constitutions. Add to this the unhealthy and almost 
loathsome, food which was doled out in little to the 
greater portion of them, and it will appear surprising ° 
that any of them could have survived it longer than 
a few years. Their ordinary nourishment consisted 
of bad bread, gruel, rancid oil, and olives; and it 
was only by a little extra work after the hardships of 
the day that they could even earn enough to purchase 
a little wine or a few other trifling comforts. They 
were divided into two portions, those which belonged 
to private individuals and those which appertained 
to the state. The former were better off in many 
respects than the latter, (it being of course to the 
interest of the master to make the most of his pro- 
perty) and were in gome cases allowed to open taverns 
or coffee houses provided they paid a certain annual 
tribute to their owners, Nay, so well did some of 
them thrive as publicans that an- English Captain, 
who was commissioned to bring back several of them * 
to their native country after Lord Exmouth’s bom- 
bardment, assured me that they were quite unwilling= 
to leave their lucrative employment, whereas the 
others were so overjoyed at their liberation that they 
fell down and kissed the soil the moment they landed 
among their own people. The condition of those 
who were termed public slaves was most deplorable. 
The state awarded them but one shirt, one tunic of 
linen with long sleeves, and a mantle, for the whole 
year. They slept together in a large building termed 
a “bagnio,” situated in the Bab-a-zoun * quarter,

that four other bagnios had to be erected. These '

with the unwholesome stench contributed, one might 
almost say mercifully, to thin their numbers. Where 
there was not room enough many of them had to 
sleep on the roof or in the courtyard, which, exposed

more preferable than the stifling pens in which their 
fellow-sufferers were confined. In the day-time they 
worked under the superintendence of a Bachi, who, 
being responsible for the amount of work performed 
by them, scrupled not to use the lash at the slightest 
meappearance of idleness or fatigue. Their ransom, too, 
was a matter of difficulty even to those who were 
provided with means sufficient for that purpose. 
Being public property no especial owner could be 
found for them, and in many cases the State refused 
to give them up. Even when the ransom of private 
slaves was attempted there were so many dues to be 
paid to the Caftan of the Pasha, Secretary of State, 
Captain of the port, Bachi, and Guardian of bagnio, 
besides other minor officers, that the original sum 
demanded was often doubled in this way befere the 
unfortunate captive could be freed. To giv an idea

It seémed a sad reproach to Christendont that so

few successful attempts at liberation were made after

so that at the first appearance of a Christian man-of- 
war they might steal out in the dead of night and 
endeavor to reach it. One evening a large ship was 
discovered on the horizon, and imagining that their 
plans were ripe they descended into the cave. To 
their horror they found a party of Janissaries coolly 
awaiting their appearance, by whom of course they 
were delivered up to Hassan Pasha, the Dey. Pos- 
terity will scarcely believe that the plan was defeated 
by the perfidy of one Of the Spanjards, who, stating 
that he wished to embrace Islamism informed the 
Dey of the projected escape and received his freedom. 
But so struck was Hassan Pasha with the nobility of 
soul displayed in the face of the illustrious slave that 
instead of inflicting condign punishment he purchased 
him of his former owner. The Spanish government, 
however, ashamed at the capture of so noble a subject, 

sent money for his redemption, and he was happy 
enough to end his days among his own people.

Towards the end of the fourteenth. century the 
convent boasted a superior in the person of Sybille 
de Foz, a young lady on the pleasant side of five and. 
twenty, but who was evidently better fitted for the 
ball room than the chilly cloister. This juvenile 
abbess had the prettiest dimple and most bewitching 
blue eye that ever peeped from beneath the con- 
ventual hood, and would altogether have-been a 
most dangerous acquaintance to carnally-disposed 
men. bove all things she was passionately fond 
of practical jokes, which she never hesitated to play 
at the expense of others, provided she could indulge 
that silvery laugh of hers which trilled through the 
frowning arches like the softest treble notes of a. flute. 
There were not wanting malicious detractors in the 
shape of ugly members of the sisterhood who af- 
firmed that although she tied herself to the bed-post 
and inflicted with hér own pretty hand a daily self- 
scourging by way of a religious constitutional yet 
the end of the lash always managed to fall on the 
wooden post instead of the white flesh, notwithstand-- 
ing a few charming screams and tears which should 
have given proof to the contrary. It was alleged, 
too, by the said detractors, that the humility and 
patience with which she devoured salt fish on Wed- 
nesdays and Fridays might be traced, not to the 
fervor of religious enthusiasm, but to the little 
piquant dressings or nicely-flavored egg-sauces which 
rendered it more palatable, and thus did away with 
the intended mortification. Whether these allegations 
werestrue or false must of course remain buried in 
the depths of bygone ages, and it behoves us, her 
chroniclers, ¢o regard her now in the charitable light

of a well-meaning though rather flirty superior, who

a-days—by interest.

The Pirates of the present day are, however, a relic 
of the “old school” that are fast dying out and will 
be extinct in a few years. They generally adopt the 
trade of boatmen? by which they gain a maintenance 
for their declining years, and are able at the same 
time to practice a few of their old tricks upon the 

“Pockets of the unfortunate passengers whom they 
may ferry to land. But the exciting privilege of the 
sea chase is taken from them for ever, and even were 
they in a condition to Tevive it there is a Giant, in- 
cubated from the wave, whose niighty power would 
render fusile the united efforts of a legion of their

The position of our Consul before the capture was

reside in*the town his residence was situated about

popular festivals, such for example as thé Bairam, it 
was unsafe for him or for any Christian to be seen 
about as the public excitement often degenerated 
into fanaticism. He wos liable, toos to seizure at any 
Moment, as was proved when he and his family were 
thrown into prison at the time of the memorable 
Exmouth bombardment. In one of the walls of the 
present British consulate (a fine Moorish tenement, 
though situated in an extremely narrow, dirty street) 
are still to be seen the remains of a missile projected 
by the British fleet, which is about the only trace 
they have left of their signal victory, and is at the 
same time a proof of the moderation of the conquering 
Union Jack. And in the’ person of the Consul *

‘A thorough or even a slight knowledge pt Arabic 
would be a most useful appendage to a visit in Algeria. 
There are so many ancient inseriptions® within the 
Mosques, on the tombstones, and over the doors of 
.private houses, that one is naturally led to-deplore 
an ignorance which is so great a bar to an immense 
deal of useful and curious information. Independent 
of this would it be a pleasure to be able to decipher 
the various placards printed in excellent type and 
stuck about the town as plefitifully as bills on an 
English “house in chancery.” One would be able 
also to gain a great deal of insight into the private 
character of the Algerines, and to make acquaintance 
with many of their proverbs and sayings, some of 
which are of the greatest antiquity. Let not the 
enthusiast content himself with the idea that French 
will serve his stead in an intercourse with the natives; 
far from it. Very few of the Algerines can speak 
that language perfectly, and even when they can 
they would not care to divulge their secret opinions 
in a tongue whose associations aré naturally hateful 
Tthem. But speak to them if you can in their own 
dialect, and ten chances to one but you will have 
gained at once the entrance to their innermost hearts. 

The Arab and Moorish boys are remarkably fine 
handsome little fellows, and quickly develop under 
the influence of that southern sun. The dress of the 
latter is exactly the same as that of their fathers, the 
“toga virilis” consisting in the assumption of the 
turban, which is wound round the fez. It*is most 
absurd sometimes to watch children who can scarcely 
walk teddling along like miniature men, thetr red 
fezzes coveging heads as cleanly shaved a$ those of

their pwgenitors. * Their games, like those of our 
own youth, are for the most part athletic, and you 
may often see them playing at a species of “hide and 
seek,” or racing frantically through some of the lonely 
old streets where a false step would caiftse a severe 
injury. Whether it is on account of the prevalence 
of the guttural element in their language, or whether 
they are really more loquacious than European chil- 
dren it is impossible to say, but at all evénts they 
seem to make a prodigious noise and hubbub when 
they are engaged in conversation, The names com- 
monest amongst them are Mohammed, Hassan, and 
Hamet, which you often hear vociferated in the same 
style as that in which the British snob bawls after 
imaginary “ Bills” or “Jims.” Give a stentorian 
shout for * Bill” in a Tandon alley and you will

an effect which the vociferation of “ Mohammed ”

I must not forget to mention an anecdote concerning 
an artist who was imprudent enough to attempt to 
sketch a full coffee-house one evening. Scarcely had 
he put his pencil to his paper before the musicians 
rushed with their instruments into the street and 
were quickly followed*by the customers, the coffee- 
house being emptied in an instant. With beetling 
brows the host approached him, and asked what he 
meant by sending off his constituents in that wag’. 
And it was only by very civil words, backed by a 
bagsheesh, that he became pacified and allowed the 
artist to depart unmolested on condition of never 
again repeating the offence. 

The Algerines are passionately fond of games of 
skill, which they play in the public thoroughfares 
surrounded in most cases by a large crowd who 
generally seem to look forward with astonishing 
interesé and patience to the result. There is one 
game which is very common and is played in the 
following manner. A number of pieces of wood 
slightly holloyed on one side and plane of the other, 

12 


172 THE CORSAIR . 


a little bigger than spellicans, are thrown up, and ac- 
cording to the flat or hollow part on which they fall 
their value is estimated, a rough board divided into 
nine equal squares being the indicator. Pieces of 
orange peel‘ and stones, which represent different 
ratios of value, indicate each time the amount gained, 
which is duly registered on the red lines that border 
the squares, and the victory is of course to the one 
who is able to mark the greatest number. Spanish 
cards, too, are a favorite amusement, but the contest 
in which they evince most interest is a species of 
draughts, played upon o board on which the squares 
are distinguished by being raised and depressed, and 
it is probably from this that our idea of black and 
white squares first originated. Chess I have never 
seen among them, although we are taught to believe 
that it is to the Arabs that we are indebted for this 
ancient and scientific amusement. Perhaps, however, 
the fact of its requiring a certain amount of mental 
labour will I think be sufficient apology for its absence 
among the Algerines, as they aré rarely anxious for 
any recreation which gives them much trouble. They 
have not lost, however, their passionate attachment to 
tales of the marvellous, and will sit and listen for 
hours to the stupidest narrator, provided he has 
something out of the common for their pricked up 
ears. The way in which the story-teller generally 
delivers himself of his budget of fables is remarkable 
for its tediousness. Every sentence is spun out with 
expressions equivalent to “says he,” “you know,” &c. 
interluded with a frightful amount of useless conjunc- 
tions and prepositions, and the description, titles, or 
name of thé hero or heroine recounted. each time in

case thS audience should lose the point of the narrative. 
The sentences in which the characters are made to 
express their feelings are of course flowing with all 
the metaphors that the narrator can string together, 
which are, as may be supposed, strangely tedious and 
irrelevant at times. An Englishman attempting to

He was declaiming in a sort of chant, while after

was heightened by a chorus from the united instru-

of the young Arab who pined so terribly for ¢ damsel 
with whom he was not allowed intercourse that his 
body after a time cast no more shadow, and who at 
length in spite of his remarkable thinness overcame 
every obstacle and eventually married her. Whether 
the amorous young gentieman put himself under a

generally state, but that I sunpose must be taken

Camaralzaman and Badoura, or tickling the adven- 
turous spirit of the Algerine youth by the marvellous 
fortunes of Sindbad the sailor. But into whatever 
sphere of romance or poesy he might have caused 
them to wander he took care ever and anon to recall 
them to earth by a tap of his tomtom, in order that 
in their small but ungrudged contributions he might 
receive the just meed of his exertions. Let them fly 
to-the clouds after that as often as they pleased, so 
long as they descend again at the drumming of the 
tambourine. After all, he was but a man! 

From the European capitals the trade of shoe-black 
has gradually worked its way into Algiers, and nu- 
merous Jewish and Arab boys tout with their little 
apparatus as eagerly as the metropolitan shoe-brigade. 
In whatever part of the modern town you wander

I was once witness of a little scene which may I think

the terrible cry of “fire,” and it was discovered that 
the second, third, and fourth stories of a house in

a high wind. Cannon were fired so rapidly that a

had attacked the town, and looking to that of course 
as his surest refuge. The military were beat to

confined to the building in which it originated, but 
which at the same time was entirely gutted. Next

day = crowd of Frenchmen, aos a matter of course,

alleged as an excuse for his barbarity that they were

Nobody interfered; and the fellow was on the point

with me at the time, stopped him, and displayed six 
feet of threatening muscle to his astonished gaze. 
“You kicked those boys, so you must kick me, for I 
have done the same as they,” said he. The Spaniard” 
scowled, but shewed few signs of a pugilistic dispo-

followed him, and my friend stuod at the door hurling 
every epithet he could muster on his head, and calling 
him every coward that the French language could 
produce, in his loudest voice. Of course the crowd 
now turned their attention to the fracas, and thought 
it best to appear to take our part. The man seeing 
such odds against him slunk out of the shop into his 
private apartment, and my friend, after endeavoring 
in vain to draw him forth by a few endearing expres-

when he next met him,—and we retired covered with 
honor, for the crowd were loud in their praises, and 
several of them actually followed us into by streets

we returned half an hour later and found him again

As may be expected the military consider them- 
selves the aristocracy of Algeria, and look down with 
unmitigated contempt on the civilians. The latter 
class im their turn are not slow to reciprocate the 
same jealous feelings, and now and then a fracas 
oceurs which always ends to the disadvantage of the 
men of peace. A short time ago a party of officers 
intimated to the master of the Café Valentin in an 
imperious tone that, they intended to frequent his 
coffee-house, and that he was therefore expected to 
exclude all civilians without distinction, in order that 
the “cercle” might be more sclect. >A fiery newspaper 
editor, hearing of the circumstance, determined to 
present himself at the café the very next evening in 
order to make this proud prohibition of none effect. 
French editors are proverbial as being not only the 
representatives but as a matter of course the dofenders 
of the press, and the consequence of this opposition 
to military arrogance may easily be conceived. The 
literary Camelford walked into the café, and took an 
excursion next morning to a retired spot out of Al- 
giers where he had the satisfaction of receiving a 
sword-wound that laid him up for six weeks. But it 
was a salutary lesson to the officers, and the Café 
Valertin has since that time been constantly invaded 
by the sacrilegious steps of the unwarlike—myself

CHAPTER VII. 


On Ash-Wednesday the whole indigenous popula- 
tion of Algiers were in a great state of excitement, 
induced, however, not by a suddenly conceived 
reverence for an occasion so sacred, but by the 
circumstance of the most important negro ceremony 
of the whole year—that of the inauguration of the 
first bean—falling upon that day. From an early 
hour in the morning crowds uf Arabs, Moors, Ne- 
groes, Negresses, and Moorish women, dressed in 
their gayest, flocked from the Bab-el-oued end of 
the town into the country, being conducted thither 
in some cases by the little omnibuses, and preferring 
in others to make use of their own feet. Each 
Negress carried a couple of painted candles and a 
gigantic key (that of the house-door), and each 
Mauresque stuck large leaves inside her hhaik by 
way of embellishment to her eyes, but which had 
at the same time a strong resemblance to blinkers. 
Curious to behold heathen rites performed within 
sight of a railway which was actually commenced 
I jumped into one of the little vehicles, and was 
soon whirling like a fire-engine along the dusty 
Littoral. ‘ 


AND HIS CONQUEROR, 181 


I myst say my fingers itched with the desire of 
making a sketch of my fellow-passengers, of whom 
the following was the compliment. Opposite me sat - 
a Moorish lady whose hands were dyed all over with 
col, the finger-nails alone being tinged with henna. 
Now and then the intense,heat compelled her to open 
for an instant the hhaik in which she was enveloped, 
and an embroidered corset richly decorated with 
flowers was exposed. Next to her was seated her 
husband, a Moor; and close to me were three at- 
tendant Negresses in blue check hhaiks, each with a 
painted taper in her hand. An English lady and 
gentleman made up the party; and I must not omit 
to mention an Arab, who was the driver, and a well- 
dressed Turk who sat next him on the box. We 
alighted on a rising ground just above the sea-shore 
and near the ruins of the Marabout Sidi Belal, from 
which a capital view of the ceremonics, which were 
performed immediately beneath, was to be obtained. 
Four or five marquees were pitched which were 
occupied by some Moorish families of distinction, 
but whether they came with a view of witnessing 
the rites is rather doubtful, as the tents were kept 
tightly closed the whole time, and the fair occupants, 
I believe, amused themselves with little else than 
devouring kous-kous. On a conspicuous flag were 
engraved in white letters on a blood red ground the 
device '‘ Vive la France, abolition de l’esclavage,” a 
wholesome way of reminding the assembled Negroes 
of the state of bondage in which but a few years 
previously they had been merged, and at the same time 
of calling to their mind the debt of gratitude they 
owed to their deliverers. The Europeau spectators, 


182 THE CORSAIR 


for whom chairs had in many instances been provided, 
stood on the above-mentioned rising ground, and on 
the slope sat the Moorish women cross-legged, to 
the number of two or three hundred, and looking 
in the distance like so many sacks of flour stored 
in rows. On the sand below sat a large circle of 
Negroes and Negresses, the latter being clothed 
in white sacrificial vestments, and the former half 
naked with a fillet of upright feathers around their 
heads. Shortly after our arrival the ceremonies 
commenced. Two powerful Negresses plunged into 
the sea, and swimming out cast the water high in 
the air towards the assembly. ‘Then half-a-dozen 
men within the circle beat drums with drumsticks 
of curved cane, and the same number jingled each a 
pair of double iron castagnets, while one played a 
bamboo reed-flute, a certain regularity of measure 
being however detected in tlie barbarous hubbub. 
Before them stood a brazier from which thick white 
clouds of incense were arising, and with their faces 
thitherward three or four Negroes executed a kind of 
religious dance, which consisted in leaping frantically 
up end down, their limbs and head swinging from 
side to:side like pieces of limp flesh in time to the 
harsh music. Some of them fell after a time ex- 
hausted and rolled with frightful convulsions in the 
yielding sand, upon which the whole assemblage 
rose, joined hands, and “danced wildly around them. 
Then the orgiasts took short clubs in each hand and 
the character of the dance changed as the “clubs 
clashed against each other, and the performers 
jomped madly up and down en masse. It was just 
the scene tuat Robinson Crusoe might have been 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 183 . 


supposed to witness when he peeped from his hiding 
place ang beheld the cannibals after they had picked 
the bones of their unfortunate brethren. Apropos of 
the subject, how melancholy must have been the 
thoughts of poor young Robinson as hé pinched his 
flesh and wondered whether he should ever live to be 
underdone! Their frantic movements were continued 
for upwards of an hour, and then a bull wreathed 
with flowers was led by the horns once or twice 
around, followed by three or four gaunt high priest- 
esses and the musicians, the procession being closed 
with the now jaded dancers. One of the high priest- 
esses was of enormous height, measuring not less 
than six feet two, and I need not observe that many 
men looked short by her side. Three cocks were’ 
first sacrificed and the omens examined. These 
being found to be propitious a shrill note of joy 
issued from the worfien, in whig¢h the Mauresques

by the gigantic priestess, the attendant priestesses

the air announced that for the second time the omens

aloft the sacrificial knife, and with muttered prayers

- 184 THE CORSAIR 


from the centre and swam for a short distance into 
the sea, casting as before the water high above their 
heads towards the land. The ceremony was now 
concluded, the bull was cut up, and a fire was lighted 
upon the spot over which it was roasted and eaten. 
And not the least picturesque part of the scene was 
a party of mounted Spakes in their red cloaks, cur- 
vetting about on the sand, and forcing their horses 
to take leaps that would have dismounted any but an 
Arab, for the purpose I suppose of parading their 
horsemanship before the impassive “sacks” within 
which it was so difficult to believe that women, ac- 
cording to our ideas of the term, weré concealed. 
According to the account of the Negroes, who 
-seem to know little more concerning their ceremonies 
than the mere name, and perform their parts almost 
like automata, the sacrifice was instituted for three 
reasons. Firstly to propitiate the powers of earth 
and air; secondly in honor of the Ethiopian Sidi 
Belal; and thirdly to inaugurate the first bean of 
the season, no matter how premature, that is dis- 
covered to be ripe. Sidi Belal, according to the 
legend, was the slave and afterwards muezzin of 
Mohammed, and being liberated by him emigrated 
to this part of Barbary, where he became so eminent 
for his piety that 2 Mosque was erected over his re- 
mains, a few whitewashed walls being all that now 
point out the sacred spot. Little or no connection 
seems to exist between the triple objects of the 
sacrifice, and their incongruity can only be récon- 
ciled by the merest conjecture. Sidi Belal may 
have been the first to have introduced the peculiar 
yellow beane into Algeria which forms so staple an 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 185 


article‘of consumption among the native races, and 
by a simple union of ideas his soul might even have 
been supposed after death to have taken up its resi- 
dence therein. Pythagoras had a most respectable 
connection among cereals, and his discijles were far 
too wise to burden their consciences with the notion 
of having devoured their grandmothers merely for 
the pleasure of eating a dish of beans.* The Mo- 
hammedans are firm adherents to the “doctrines of 
transmigration, and every bird or beast, that ap- 
proaches a Mosque or Marabout is supposed to be 
animated with the spiritual essence of departed 
relations, and regarded therefore as sacred. The 
same superstition consecrates as a matter of course 
the vegetable creation, and every tree or shrub in 
holy places is tenanted by its appropriate spirit. The 
powers of earth and air, in the idea of the intimate 
connection of those elements with, the ripening and 
developing of the fruit of the soil, are doubtless 
supplicated for their propitious influence upon the 
bean-harvest, and the answer of the unknown deities, 
favorable or not, would naturally be expressed in the 
disposition of the omens. The splashing of sea- 
water towards the sacrificers is evidently intended 
as a lustration, the purifying propensities of salt, 
whether crystallized or in solution, being too well 
known to need comment. No precepts with regard 
to these sacrifices are mentioned in the Koran; and 
although the Negroes who perform them are strict 
Mussutmen yet no other portion of the indigénes

ever take part in them. Of their immense sntiquity

under the assumed guise of a barbarous devotion to 
the Marabout, himself a slave and naturally the 
patron saint of slaves, till in the course of centuries 
the real object of the sacrifice was lost and the spirit 
gave way by degrees to the modern letter. The 
French seem to regard them however in the spirit- 
less light of a later invention, an idea which seems 
of little weight when the striking character of their 
features are taken into consideration, as the incapacity 
of the negro crushed by ages of oppression is well 
known; and there seems little reason why a slavish 
population should have purposely designed innova- 
tions which, militating against the precepts of Mo- 
hammed, (in which idolatry is so strictly forbidden) 
would as a matter of course bring upon them the 
wrath of their bigoted masters.

above our heads and then die and melt away in the

who were obliged to ‘adopt this extraordinary mode 
of witnessing the ceremonies, their presence among 
men being of course inconsistent with the principles 
of Islamism. The note they utter consists in the 
monosyllable “lu,” repeated over and over again, 
and enunciated in the highest tone of which the 
female voice is capable, and is used as a sign of 
approbation, pleasure, or pain, according to cireum- 
stances. And women are hired either at marriages or 
funerals whose sole profession consists in leading this 
weird chorus at the very top of their shrill voices.

fig, grows round Algiers into a regular tree of twelve 
feet or so in height, and the leaves are of course large 
in proportion, being generally about a foot long and 
half an inch thick, and are very thickly covered with 
strong prickles of an inch in length. These prickles 
are as thick as a drugget-pin at the base, and very 
firm, so that the handling of the Igaf is a matter 
of difficulty and pain, and should the point of the 
prickle break in so doing it forces itself beneath the 
skin and causes excruciating agony. The Arabs 
crawled adroitly towards the man who held the 
leaves, baying like the dogs they imitated, and as 
he held one forth they thrust their heads forward 
and took rapid bites, devouring it seemingly without 
the slightest inconvenience. The green fluid ex 
pressed from the herb flowed in streams over their 
long beards, and I noticed that when they acciden- 
tally touched each other they gave a low growl like

curs who are gorging. The applause of the invisible 
ladies was great, but by no means so enthusiastic as 
before, the tambourines were again silent and the 
performers fell to the ground as if the superhuman 
stimulus to their exertions had been reraoved. One 
of them continued insensible for so long a time that 
it was feared life was extinct. Happily, however, he 
revived, and was thus mercifully prevented from a 
death in the midst of such disgraceful orgies. 

Kous-kous was again brought round to fortify the 
enthusiasts for a repetition of the, ceremonies which 
were to be kept up till six o’clock in the morning. 
Finding that it was midnight I rose and uttered 
“‘salaam aleikum” (peace to you) to the assembly, 
which salutation was gravely returned by “salaama” 
(good bye). I drew a deep breath as I left the house, 
like one who has by a timely awaking been relieved 
from the incubus ofsome terrible night-mare, and 
as I threaded again the narrow streets the delicious 
night air cooled and refreshed me. The Place du 
Gouvernment was now deserted, but there were the 
same fragrant orange trees, the same tideless sea, 
and to the right the same eternal snows of the ma- 
jestic Atlas, which seemed by their very calmness to 
‘reproach me for the burning flush which the excite- 
ment of those dark idolatrous rites had brought to 
my cheeks. No wonder that my sleep was broken 
and disturbed that night.

CHAPTER VIII. 


For a considerable time great doubt existed in the 
minds of antiquaries as to the appellation with which 
Algiers was supposed to be invested by its ancient 
conquerors. Some contended for the correctness of 
the term Jomnium, while others again on the au- 
thority of Pomponius Mela declared that Icosium 
was the more likely expression. A recently disco- 
vered inscription has however, if reliance can be 
placed on it, settled the long disputed point.* 

A poor French nail-maker who rented a small 
Moorish house in the Rue Bab-a-zoun brought to 
light from the depths of a long closed up cellar a 
moderately sized. square stone, the convenient smooth- 
ness of which immediately suggested to him the idea 
of transforming it into a species‘ of anvil for his nails. 
He acted on the suggestion, and at his death the 
owner of the-house built it up in the wall over a shop 
now occupied by a barber. An antiquary, passing 
by, noticed some letters engraved thereon, and having 
obtained permission to cleanse it from a thick couting 
of dust discovered the only inscription in which the 


* Dr. Shaw is of opinion, however, that Icosium was situa-ed on the 
banks of the Ha:ash, four miles to the eastward of Algiers. 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 207 


ancient name is mentioned. He immediately offered 
a reward for the stone, which would not have been 
difficult to detach at the time as the building was 
not nearly completed, but in vain, the owner not 
being willing to put himself to any trouble on the 
subject. At the, suggestion of the antiquary the 
authorities of the museum put themselves in commu- 
nication with him, but with no better success, and 
like a veritable dog in the manger he, completed the 
construction of his house, and the stone still remains 
as a monument of his ignorant selfishness. The 
inscription is as follows :-—* is

208 THE CORSAIR 


Hercules in his passage along this part of the coast 
was abandoned by twenty of his followers, who chose 
out a situation for themselves and laid the founda- 
tions of a future town. <A contention having arisen 
among then, however, as to which of their names 
the town should bear they came to the conclusion of 
bestowing a title on it which should remind distant 
generations of the number of its founders; without 
alluding to apy one of them in particular. Accor- 
dingly from the word exooe (eikosi, twenty) was 
derived Eikosion, which, by the Roman conquerors, 
was Latinized into the form Icosium. 

Very little mention of it is made however by the 
Latin authors. Pliny tells us that it received the 
Roman franchise, which proves that it must have 
been a town of some little importance, derivable 
perhaps from its being a convenient port for ingress 
and egress as regards the interior, or from its form- 
ing a station on the great coast road to Mauritania 
Tingitensis. The traces of a Roman road have been 
discovered in the Bab-a-zoun and Bab-el-oued streets, 
and ‘likewise in the Rue de la Marine, and through 
the two former the highway to the Eastern and 
Western districts most probably passed, while from 
the Rue de la Marine ran the road perhaps that led 
into the interior. The latter street, to judge from the 
number of broken columns which were excavated 
from it during its reconstruction by the French, 
must have formed the principal thoroughfare of the 
city, especially as it is highly probable that it‘led, 
as now, to the port, and would be the first to be 
traversed by those who entered the town from the 
sea. Icosium. is said to have possessed Bishops at 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 209 


the Christian era, but their names are unfortunately 
lost to us. The Barbarossas when they founded the 
Turkish dominion in Algiers greatly enlarged the 
city, and filled it with public monuments which not 
only absorbed the ruins of Icosium but’ also those 
of other smaller Roman towns that existed in its 
vicinity. Hence the number of the relics that have 
come to us is of necessity small, and they have been 
mostly discovered among the foundations of demol- 
ished Moorish houses. Many objects of antiquity, 
including some valuable manuscripts, were unfor- 
tunately lost in consequence of the non-existence of 
a museum during the first few years of the conquest. 
That glaring defect has, however, been now repaired, 
and there is much Within the present musée to repay 
the antiquary for the trouble of a visit. The principal 
objects of interest consist mostly in numerous slabs 
covered with inscriptions as deep and fresh in many 
instances as when they issued from the chisel of the 
sculptor, but too often remarkable for the provincial 
character ef the Latin. Many of the letters, too, are 
of an ornamental kind, and bear no distant resem- 
blance to the flourishing capitals which adorn the 
shops in our own public thoroughfares. There are 
several gravestones with figures of the deceased, in 
relief, who are in most instances represented in the 
act of holding fruit, such as grapes, ‘in the right 
hand. I was rather astonished at seeing many of 
these figures surmounted by a crescent, which stands 
out Likewise in relief. On examination, however, it 
differs from the Turkish emblem inasmuch as it 
presents a much smaller segment with regard to its 
outer circumference, having more the appearance and 


210 THE CORSAIR 


dimensions of a half-moon than that greater circum- 
ference by which the true crescent is supposed to be 
distinguished. It may perhaps have been employed 
as the symbol of ‘Proserpine or Hecate, in her office 
of arbitress of the realms below, as it was of Diana 
or Luna, and might have been placed there in a 
kind .of tague religious hope of propitiating the 
goddess, or possibly simply as an ornament consistent 
with the creed of the true Roman. . Within one of 
the half-moons a star was conspicuous, but I should 
be disposed to attach little weight to its appearance 
there, as it is not only not in relief but very roughly 
carved, and might easily have been added afterwards 
by a Mohammedan ignorant of the real nature of 
the-symbol which he was thus embellishing. Seve- 
ral very perfect busts and statues have also come to 
light, and many large amphors of a size capable of 
containing with ease two or three men. There is a 
large sarcophagus, too, which might have held either 
the remains of a Pagan or Christian physician from 
the nature of the bas-relief on the exterigr. These 
consist of several figures, one of which is represented 
with a cock perched at its feet, and might be taken 
equally for a representation of St. Peter or Asculapius. 
The lead of the interior coffin is nearly perfect, and 
through 4 small fissure can be perceived the unmuti- 
lated skeleton of the occupant. A curious hand-mill 
has also been dug up, and a Sella Balnearis, or bath 
chair, of carved white marble. In the centre of the 
seat is a circular hole about a foot in diameter, 
through which it was intended either that the water 
poured upon the bather should escape, or that steam 
should pass up from beneath in the manner of 8 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 211 


vapor-bath. Both these operations may have been 
combined in the daily baths of the African colonist, 
the steam being introduced from beneath to cast him 
into a kind of languor in order that the cold water 
applied afterwards in the manner of ashower bath 
might produce a still moye delicious reaction on his 
heated body. “Then he might be rubbed down and 
anointed afterwards, and finally left to repose, reeking 
with. perfume, and dream away the time that preceded 
the evening meal, “This would not differ greatly in 
detail from the present Moorish ‘batlis, the delightful 
effect of which I have been assured few earthly 
sensations can surpass, though at the same time they 
. are weakening ‘even to the strongest constitutions. 
The pleasure-loving Algerines, however, both male 
and female indulge nearly every day in this delicious 
though debilitating practice, which in some instances 
has actually been known to produce hydrocephalus. 
To suppose that the present Algiers was built

by the Berbers, Mosgans, or Beni Mezarhanna. At

For nearly thirty years the French have held Al- 
geria at the bayonet’s point. For an equivalent 
period so xtany millions of francs have been annually 
expended by them for the maintenance of the mis- 
called colony, which is useful in few other points 
than as a training school for recruits. Like the 
Romans, of whom their proud boast is to be the 
modern representatives, they @n raise great works 
and keep the native population in subjection, but 
colonists in the thorough signification of the word 
they are not, and can never be. There is wanting 
the sturdy patience and sanguine endurance of the 
British husbandman, so opposed to the fiery and 
volatile spirit of the Frenchman, who can hardly 
bear to wait for the results of his industry, and who 
loses heart at the first failuwe. Perhaps a cogent 
reason may be adduced for the ill success of the 
colony in the fact that very few capitalists are wil- 
ling to exchange the pleasures and conveniences of 
their mother-country for a sojourp in a relaxing 
climate, with a by no means clear-sighted view of 
success, and abovesg}l that feeling of exile with which 
every Frenchman who is long absent from his own 
fertile land is invariably oppressed. Hence it is only 
poor and méserable men who have tried their hands at 
every expedient, but without success, who claim a 
free passage and emigrate to Algeria as the Ultima 
Thule of their expectations. The enotmous custom- 
house and port duties, too, preclude to a certain

To quote Fisquet again (who wrote at the time 
when Algeria had but partially succumbed to the 
arms of the Gaul) ‘That which we want before 
everything else is a colonization, solid and durable 
as that of the Romans, and when once that coloniza- 
tion is founded the whole of Algeria wifl be our 
absolute property.” It must, however, be many 
years of patience and industry,bofore the colony will 
be sufficiently prosperous even to defray its own cost; 
and on referring to the above budget the array of 
expenses produce a’ melancholy counter-balance to 
the revenues. Fresh works have to be constructed 
yearly, and fresh roads to be made, on which many 
millions of francs must yet be expended, while at the 
same time the maintenance of a huge standing army 
will be, till the native population lose their ever 
burning desire for independence—an event that will

One broiling hot day towards the end of April we 
had made up a small picnic party to visit the roman- 
tic environs of Bir-Madrais,—Anglicé “Well of the 

‘aptain’s rest.”” Ladies are by no means uncomfort-

was impossible to murmur as our little omnibus flew 
along the dusty read of Mustwpha Superior. After 
about a couple of hours’ driving we alighted in a 
delightfully wooded valley, and beneath the shadows 
of wilg orange and lemon trees spread our cloth, the 
thickly growing flowers forming excellent cushioned 
seats for the fair portion of the guests. 

Now garrulous#engued Report had been nearly 
driving us out of our senses a few days previously 
by the announcement that a quarry had been dis- 
covered, which, by the numerous figures and inscrip-

the Romans. In an unhappy moment I stole away 
with three companions, and after a brisk Walk we

So after a little debate I volunteered an entrance, 
and crawled carefully in on my hands and feet. 
After proceeding a little way the roof became so 
low that I could not have gone further éxcept -by

Nothing can indicate the fondness of Frenchmen 
for grand sounds more powerfully than the above- 
mentioned title of “Genie Militaire.” With them a 
rose would scarcely emat as sweet a fragrance under a 
different appellation in spite of old Shakespeare’s 
notions to the contrary, unless that name was some- 
thing that would elevate the scent of the flower into 
a sort of spirituglized attar, so that they might be 
able to speak of it as “Vesprit florale.” What's in 
a name? The Frenchman angwers “ everything.” 
Ask him why twenty-franc pieces are spoken of as 
Napoleons, instead of the ancient title of Louis d’or? 
Ask him why the terrified townspeople addressed each 
other as “citoyen” in the bloody days of the great 
revolution, and why all degrees of kith and kin were 
Merge into that unmeaning epithet? Ask him why 
the dirtiest rag and bone shop is dignified by the 
title of “ magasin,’ ? and why the small grocer round 
the cornér would consider it an alarming wyeach of 


238 THE CORSAIR 


etiquette if you omitted,to address him, in common

if he gives you that wonderfully expressive shrug of 
the shoulders, which Frenchmen alone can give, and 
which is intended to convey to your mind anything 
or nothing as you please to take it, conclude your 
queries by asking him why the working portion of 
the army whose province it is to build barracks and 
construct roads should be raised to the clouds and 
deified under the astounding dppellation of Genie 
Militaire ?” 

He will probably give another shrug and direct 
towards you an appealing look as though he had 
resigned himself to your better judgment. Whats 
world of trouble it would save Englishmen if they 
could only master that sudden elevation of the shoul- 
der bones! It is the ultimatum of the Frenchman 
from which there,is no furtker appeal, and which 
you are bound to believe contains the right answer 
to your queries if you could only unravel it from the 
Sphynz-like enigma of upturned eyeballs and down- 
ward mouth in which it is veiled. Juike the ancient 
philosophers he propounds wisdom to you under the 
guise of a riddle, agd if you cannot find the right 
solution you must lay it to your own stupidity, not 
his. What a brilliant idea for our rising generation 
if along with their present scientific acquisitions 
they could be taught the science of shrugs! What 
a helping hand out of the slough of despond of rivd 
voce examinations would be afforded by a tlerough 
acquaintance with the details of this novel accom-

T heard her snoring like a humming-top. And then

Madame Velours alleged the common excuse of 
“ennui” which sent her to bed at the early hour 
of nine. Happily the word has no equitalent in our 
practical English language, nor is its true meaning I 
am happy to say thoroughly appreciated by English 
ladies. Among the French it ig an actual disense, 
from the terror of which they vainly endeavor to fly 
_ by shortening as much as possible the hours during 
which it can make its appeareance. Th8 dolce far 
niente more accords with our notions of idleness, when, 
after the completion of some labours, we literally en- 
joy a slrdrt kind of pleasureable repose to give our 
faculties breathing time. Ennui on the contrary is 
the evil demon which harasses all classes in France, 
from the duchess down to the blanchisseusO The

The excuse of the Arabs for drinking absinthe is 
rather ingenious, and savours strongly of Jesuitism. 
The Koran, they say, prohibits the use of wine, but 
as absinthe is not wine therefore; by the simplest 
reasoning, there can be no harm in indulging in it. 
In this way, if we may believe the French statements, 
they elude many other precepts, even those enjoining 
hospitality, upon, which, although a Mussulman may 
pride himself, yet there are numerous cases in which 
the idea of ordinary charity dogg not seem to enter 
his head. The following anecdote from the pages 
of Fisquet would convey a very different notion as to 
the “nobility of mind” which we are accustomed to 
associate with the wild and free inhabitant of the 
desert. A company of Arabs were returning from 
Algiers»*with their wagons and mules after a day’s 
pleasure there. After a few miles march one of them 
accidentally slipped down beneath a wagon, the heavy 
wheel of which passed over his legs and figctured
