244 THE CORSAIR 


them both. In this melancholy condition he besought 
his companions to set him on a mule and take him 
back to the town. None of them caring however to 
take the trouble of going over the same ground again, 
he’ was left, wounded and helpless as he was on the 
ground, each as they passed him uttering the Phari- 
saical comfort “God is great. » “One would feel very 
sceptical as to the truth of the above anecdote, ema- 
nating as it does from the pen of a Frenchman who 
abuses every nation right and Beft save his own, and 
discovers thieves and pickpockets in the lines’of every 
foreign face that passes. There is no vice that he 
does not lay at the door of the unfortunate indigénes 
of Algeria, and with the same gall-tipped pen actually 
goes so far as to assign bad motives for the few good 
deeds of which he will allow them to be the doers. 
Most inconsistently he affirms that their almsgivings 
do not proceed fgom compassion to the object, but 
from a slavish obedience to the precepts of their 
religion. For comparative savages what more could 
M. Fisquet desire? But what he looks on as their 
greatest crime is the narrowness of their affection, 
which. he bitterly complains does not extend beyond 
their family circles,and has no tinge of nationality 
in it. Whether the French affection for the Common-

the executioner—the glorious idea of libérté, eyalité, 
JSraternité—is an example to be recommended to the

marks of a “Grand Revolution.” 7

AND HIS CONQUEROR. 245 


the word Arab militates greatly against the character 
for bravery and openness which we are accustomed to 
assign tothem. The original meaning of the Hebrew 
verb ANN (Arab) may be rendered by the phrase “he 
laid snares”’—an expression which accords wonder- 
fully even at the present-day with their treacherous 
method of warfare, as they rarely if possible shew 
themselves, but are continually taking secret advan- 
tages of their enemies, or falling unawares upon the 
ill-protegted caravans of the merchants. “In the 
way hast thou sat for them like an Arabian in the 
wilderness,” saith Jeremiah, chap. iii. v. 2. and our 
translators have given a much more effective meaning 
than if-they had chosen to adopt the more literal 
version and rendered it “like he laid snares in the 
desert.”” Some have proposed the alteration “like a 
thief in the wilderness,” wherein the grammar and 
meaning would be viofated, first én converting the 
verb into a noun, and secondly in assuming the 
derivation of thief from the idea of one who laid 
snares. Altogether it seems probable that it.would 
apply more to their insidious habits as combatants 
than their equally wily practices as marauders.. The 
original derivation, however, oféhe term Arab does 
not seem to belong to the Hebrew verb, but to 
Yarab, the son of Kahtan, their reputed ancestor, 
who gave his name to Araba, a small territory in, the 
province of Tehama, where Ishmael was afterwards 
Supposed to dwell. 

The Gress of the Arab is the most simple it is 
Possible to conceive. A tunic with short sleeves 
(gandoura) almost the exact counterpart of that 


Worn by the Romans, a girdle, generally Phe 


246 THE CORSAIR 


end of which answers the purpose of a purse, bagged 
trowsers, and a garment which covers the top of his 
head, being wrapped round it by cords of black 
camel’s hair, and descends to the knees. This is the 
hhaik, and must not be confounded with the burnous, 
the form of which is tooewell known in England to 
need description. Of the real African burnouses but 
few find their way to our chilly island, the generality 
of those which are sold under that title being manu- 
factured at Lyons. Nay, many of the Lygns bur- 
nouses are actually exported to Algeria, and as they 
are much cheaper than the original manufactures, of 
which the greatest portion are imported from Tunis, 
are of course in proportionate request among the na- 
tive population. Hence good-natured though stingy 
Englishmen who take presents of cheap burnouses to ' 
our own fair dames of Albion must not be surprised 
to find an exact ecounterpar? of the article in the 
large magasins of the Rue Rivoli. 

If any of the Algerine indigénes must be accused 
of a large lack of the milk of human kindness it is 
the Moors. From the earliest time they have been 
famed for their treachery, of which no more striking 
example can be add®&ed than that of King Bocchus, 
who received part of Mauritania as his reward, for 
delivering up Jugurtha to the Romans. Oppressed 
too by the dominant Turks their bad qualities soon

An old Moor, to the surprise of every one, married, 
one fine day, a young girl of thirteen, in addition to 
a large family of wives and concubines. ‘ What 
could have induced you to take that step?” asked a 
Frenchman of him. “She was left to my charge a 
penniless orphan,” replied the Moor, “and as I didn’t 
exactly know what to do with her—I married her.” 
The answer of the Moor might convey a useful hint 
to the myriad old bachelors with money who turn so 
deaf an ear to the importunities of extremely doubtful 
aged unmarried ond lonely ladies.

mortification of feeling that he was dishonored, and

The early development of the Barbary races ren- 
ders boys marriageable at thirteen and girls at ten. 
Matrimonial affectiqn is of gourse out of the question, 
nor is it considered requisite where wives are such 
utter articles of traffic, and where a mere dislike is 
sufficient ground on the part of the Itisband to ob- 
tain a divorce. The’ lady, however, is by no means 
deprived of the chances of another and more success- 
ful marriage, for instances have been known where 
Moorish women have been united to three or four 
different husbands in succession, all of whom were 
living at the same time. The dowry, too, that she 
brings to her first husband is, in case of a divorce, 
transferred to the second, and so on till the lady is 
finally settled for life: Should .the first husband 
change his mind after he has put away his wife he 
cannot recal her unless she be married in the interval 
to another man, and a Moor will not unfrequently in 
that case employ,a friend to represent a husband for 
four and twenty hours and then give the bride up to 
her prior lord. If, however, the husband by proxy 
admires the lady he may very coolly take possession 
of her himself, a proceeding from which no article of 
the Mohammedan law can hinder him* Moorish 
ladies, in spite of the yashmak which invariably 
conceals their beauties from the gaze of the vulgar, 
are by no means indisposed to swallow a little fresh 
air when they find themselves in a secluded spot.

suddenly came upon a party who were seated unveiled

A tribe of roving Berbers settled for » few days in

Visages presented coarser and fiercer lineaments than

Contrary, too, to the custom of the Algerine women

The term Berber, as well as the appellation of 
Barbary, is by many philologers supposed to be de- 
rived from the imitative native cry Barbar, applied, 
at the period when the Greeks and Asiaticy might 
probably have used a common idiom, to the ruder 
tribes whose pronunciation and grammar might 
from the want of culture have become defective. 
Hence in later ages the epithet “ Barbarous” com- 
prehended gradually in the elegant Greek mind all 
that appertained not to itself, the Romans themselves 
in tho earlier period of the empire not being exempt 
trom the contemptuous title. According to their own 
accounts, however, the Berbers deduce their origin 
from Berber, whom they allege to have been the son 
of Mazigh, grandson of Ham, son of Noah, and 
from whom as a natural sequence the whole coast- 
line of Barbary acquired its name. The existence of 
Berber, of whom there is no mention either in the 
Old Testament or Koran, may easily have been taken 
for granted by the Kabyles, in order to explain away, 
through the uncertain medium of a legend, the 
otherwise vexatious sobriquet by which they are still 
recognized; but with regard to the term Mazigh a 
greater shadow of probability exists in the idea that 
it might be an alteration of, or substitution for, 
Mizraim, who would stand in pretty nearly the same 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 255 


relationship to Noah. Now Mizraim was the reputed 
ancestor of the Lybians and Cyrenians, of whom the 
earliest accounts name the former as having migrated 
into Numidia and Mauritania, and roved about as 
pastoral tribes subsisting on roots and the flesh of 
wild animals. Hence, assuming the above, it is by 
no means an impossible idea that the Kabyles or 
Berbers, both on account of their roving habits and 
their known antiquity, might be the actual represen- 
tatives of thoso very Lybians who, in company 
with the Getulions, were the first known colonists 
of Algerine Barbary.* 

By the Arabs the Berbers are spoken of as Kabyles, 
a word, too, which is more in vogue among Europeans. 
This term is derivable from the word K’ebail, clans or 
tribes, into which they formed themselves at the time 
of the Roman invasion, and shutting themselves up 
amid their almost iraccessible mountains proved too 
Strong for the new colonists, and hence their proud

and in the boundless expanse of the Sahara they are termed Touaregs. 
In Morocco they scarcely recognize the superiority of the Emperor, and

The hackneyed sentence “In the name of the 
prophet—figs” finds a frequent counterpart among 
the aborigines of Algeria. Whatever deed, no matter 
how small, is performed, or whatever bargain, how- 
ever insignificant, is struck, the use of “ Bishmillah”’* 
or its equivalent exclamation is invariably employed. 
“Salaam aleikum”—peace be with you—is the prays 
form of an adieu, and in answer to it “Salaama ”— 
peace to you also—is employed. ‘Washentsa ”— 
how are you—answers to our good morning, and 
the person addressed answers “Wash hhalik”’—a 
corresponding expression of courtesy with the same 
meaning as the above. “Sidi,” from which was 
doubtless taken the Spanish “Cid,” is a term equiva- 
lent to our “ Esquire,” and is applied to grandees 
only, while “Sitti” is the title by which high ladies

as the Mohammedans are not permitted to eat any- 
thing which does not lose the greater portion of its 
Vital fluid at death. 

There is one Arabic word which can be made to 
express almost everything according to the fancy of 
the speaker, and which has, as a matter of course, 
been adopted by the French till it is almost incorpo- 
rated into their languege; that is the word “makasch.” 
It has no fixed meaning save that of a general nega- 
tion, and the only approach to it will be found in 
the anglicism of the Turkish word “bosh,” and its 
schoolboy adjective “boshey.” It is as nearly as 
possible a verbal translation of the expressive French 
shrug. Supposing « dealer to,ask you too much for 
his wares you would instantly reply ‘“makasch,” 
which in that case would signify “ you’ve asked me 
too much.” When solicited as to the condition of your 
health you would reply ‘“‘makasch” if you wished to 
convey the idea that it was unfavorable. ‘“ Makasch 

m’the ”—“ that’s not good,” “makasch Inglez’”’—- 
“you're not an Englishman,” are the. commonest 
forms im which it is used. If an inferior had been 
trying to impose upon you with falsehoods you would 


260 THE CORSAIR 


merely say “makasch,” which in that sense would 
mean “you'd better shut up, you’ve only been talking 
nonsense.” In fact there is scarcely a use to which 
this flexible word may not be applied, and it is a 
thousand pities that it cannot be introduced among 
our schoolboys as a more vleasing and decidedly 
more expressive word than the uneuphonious enigma 
“ bosh.” 

“‘Makasch”’- was frequently used by the Arabs on 
the occasion of the Carnival. This in Algiers, as in 
the Italian cities, is a general holiday, and for a week 
or two before it masks parade the streets on Sundays 
to give the populace a foretaste of the mirth and 
merriment of this Roman Catholic saturnalia. On 
the grand day, however, which fell this year on 
Thursday the eighth of March, from an early hour 
in the morning the streets were given up entirely to 
the votaries of Momus. All the shops were closed, 
and even the hotel waiters had a holiday,—a thing 
almost unprecedentedamong this much enduring and 
never tiring class. At eleven o’clock every one who 
could turned out in costume, and danced grotesquely 
along the streets, eliciting loud laughs from the easily 
pleased mob. There were hideous animals, devils on 
donkey-back, powerful looking women with carica- 
tures of crinoline, Punchinellos, Harlequins, Pierrots, 
and wonderfal mounted courtiers habited according 
to their ideas of costume in the last’ century, who 
amused themselves by scattering flour and chalk 
sugar-plums on the spectators. There were several 
very good comic groups, too, which “took” uncom- 
monly well. One man carried a long bambeo, with 
a string hanging from the end in the manner of &

The Carnival was followed up at the end of March

the gentlemen shifted the best they could. I took

CHAPTER X. 

I emérged from my hotel at six o’clock on a lovely 
Morning at the beginning of March to catch the 
Blidah diligence, in whose banguette I had the 
evening before engaged a place. Although I was 
tather late I could not help pausing for a moment, as 
T ran across the Place du Gouvernment, to admire 
the magnificent appearance presented by the Sahel 
and Jurjura as they stood forth surrounded by a halo 
of glory, brilliant as that borrowed rainbow of colors 
with which the prismatic triangle of glass invests 
objects the most common-place and familiar. On 
account, too, of the wonderful clearness of the morn- 
ing air the serrated snow-peaks seemed to be brought 
at least half as near again as I had ever seen them, 
while every prominence of the blue Atlas was thrown 
into strong relief, not an atom of the indistinctness 
of numerous leagues smoothing the sharp outline of 
shadow in which the smallest fissure was veiled. 

We were soon whirling along Mustapha Superior, 
passing in our way innumerable houses, or rather 
palaces, with terraces and exterior colonnades of the 
purest Moorish architecture, once the favorite winter 
quarters of the Algerine nobility; but the greater 


272 THE CORSAIR 


portion of them now being converted into domiciles 
for rich Europeans. The country for a short distance 
presented an undulating sea of hills and valleys as 
thickly carpetted with exotics as though they formed 
a succession of nursery gardens, but after a time the 
road began to wind less and less, the increasing 
spareness of the vegetation began to indicate 2 more 
barren and marshy soil, and at length we found 
ourselves at ‘he commencement of that vast plain 
denominated the Metidja.* 

The Metidja is bounded towards the South and 
South-east by a continuous chain of mountains, and 
fertile enough in some parts for the cultivation of 
rice and Indian corn. Its general character is how- 
ever decidedly unhealthy, particularly towards the 
centre, in consequence of the fogs with which it is 
covered in the early mornings, but which vanish 
with the increased elevation of the sun. It presents 
no traces of fossit organic remains, nor have the 
bones of the larger animals been discovered there. 
It is formed of alluvial deposits, strictly horizontal, 
composed of a grey marl containing small pebbles, 
but no blocks of stone of any size, and these pebbles 
are the largest and most frequent towards the base 
of the mountains, bui diminish in size and quantity 

* The word M'tidja signifies “ arable land,” a term which corresponds 
with the fertility of that part of the plain under proper cultivation. 
Some French authors insist that it signifies “ the crowned ”—an expres- 
sion derivable of course from the chain of mountains that rise around 
it—forgetting that in the latter case it ought to be written “ Mtib’idja,” 
between the pronunciation of which and the word M’tidja the-e is an 
important difference. In Arabic wonderful difficulty is experienced in 
the very slight difference of pronunciation which will entirely alter the 


meaning of the word. Thus the term Seba with a different stress on its 
syllables signifies “a lion,” “morning,” and “seventh.” 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 273 


towards the coast. From Cape Matifou to the South- 

east, at a good distance from the shore, a line of 
small sandy downs extends, in which quantities of 
sea-shells similar to those existing at present along 
the sea-coast have been found. Taking these circum- 
stances into consideration it seems highly probable 
that at some remote period of time the Metidja might 
have formed a branch of the sea itself, the coast-line 
commencing with the Sahel and taking a South- 
westerly sweep. ‘The French reads diverge to the 
different points of communication in the Sahel, like 
the radii of a fan, the most important being that 
which leads to Blidah. So straight a road as the 
latter I never saw, not a single turn or bend oceur- 
ring in it for upwards of twenty miles, so that viewed 
from the entrance of the Metidja it has the appearance 
of a gigantio needle stretching across the plain, and 
smoothed by the distance into the sharpest imagina- 
ble point. Nothing, too, can equal the tedium 
experienced in driving along so unvaried a highway, 
for in an ordinary journey the traveller might specu-— 
late upon some change of scenery or object of interest 
which the next turn might produce, whereas in the 
Metidja he not only has the momentary dulness but 
a delicious vision of two or three good hours of 
the same changeless monotony in that yellow path, 

the termination of which is concealed by the mere 
distance. The: first village at which we arrived was 
Birkadem (well of the Negress) consisting of a few 
sickly looking houses, and a cabaret or two. There 
was a Moorish building, however, which had very 
much the appearance of a bath-house, and the portico 

of which I should very much have liked to have

sketched had the changing of the horses afforded me 
time. Away we drove along the straight road, now 
and then a tall palm tree visible for miles in the dis- 
tance whirling past us, or the cactus, which here 
grows into a tree with a trunk of twelve or thirteen 
feet in height, or the jujnbe, pepper, and carob-tree 
which now and then bounded the road-side. We 
met several parties of Arabs riding sideways, like 
women, on their mules, which walked before each 
other in Indian file, no attempts being made by the 
Arabs to converse, or in any way enliven the dulness 
of the route. In this fashion they travel from village 
to village, their only object being to learn. the latest 
news, which they leisurely retail on their return home 
over a cup of coffee to their listening neighbours. 
Except on horseback the Arab never sits astride, and 
when you see him on his donkey, his hhaik wrapped 
over his head with the black 2amel’s hair cords, and 
his bare arms flying about like the sails of a windmill, 

_ the ludicrous idea of old women in general, and Mrs. 
Gamp in particular, immediately presents itself to 
you in spite of all other thoughts. 

Our next stage was Bouffarick (Father of separa- 
tion) the high street, of which is shaded by a row of 
plane-trees, the relics of a small wood with which 
the town was formerly surrounded. The French, 
however, were compelled to cut down all the trees in 
consequence of the hiding-places affo-ded by them to 
the Arabs, who, according to their furtive manner of 
warfare, did great execution with the stray shots 
which they were enabled to discharge in comparative 
security. There is a large caravanserai here, which 
bas of course been transformed into a gendarmerie, 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 275 


and which forms the entrepét of the Metidja. A little 
beyond Bouffarick is Beni-Mered (the sons of the 
sick), a name conferred most appropriately upon it 
by the natives, in consequence of its being about the 
taost unhealthy and pestilential spot in the whole 
plain. It was at this spot that, according to the 
tenor of an obelisk erected there, twenty-two soldiers 
of the twenty-sixth regiment, under the command of 
Sergeant Blandan, fell, bravely fighting against a 
strong body of Arcbs. Beyond this point the plain 
becomes more fertile, and the vegetation both stronger 
and more abundant. At half-past twelve we ap- 
proached the town of Blidah, of which for a mile or - 
two nothing was visible save the white house-tops 
rising above a dark line of orange-groves interspersed 
With the graceful palm trees. The situation of the 
“Little Town,” as the translation of its name implies, 
is the most delicious that can be well imagined. It 
is built at the foot of one of the chain of the lesser 
Atlas, and being protected to a certain degree from 
the influence of the severe winds enjoys a climate 
far more equable than that of Algiers. The snow, 
however, lies on the top of the mountains till the 
beginning or middle of March, so that a chill is 
sometimes imparted to its wtmosphere on certain 
days of the winter months, which might be hurtful 
to invalids. The side of its mountain-protector is 
thickly covered, with vegetation, principally the fan- 
palm, while near the summit the tall tops of cedar 
forests peer from the snow, appearing from the valley 
like grains of gunpowder scattered over its white 
surface. We passed through two or three streets 
which reminded me very much of the entrance to 


276 THE CORSAIR 


Toulon, and took up our quarters at the Hotel de ls 
Regence—the Mivart’s (on a remarkably small scale) 
of Blidah. Unfortunately for us this hotel was the 
head quarters of the military, and when at five o’clock 
that evening we came in with sharpened appetites and 
ordered dinner we could scarcely get enough to eat, 
although steaming dishes were carried by every mo- 
ment to the tables of the officers. Remonstrance 
was out of the question, as the waiters assured us 
in a whispered tone of voice thet they were obliged 
to carry the best of everything to the officers, who 
would otherwise make it extremely disagreeable to 
the Maitre d’Hotel. The officers are it appears quar- 
tered to a certain degree on the hotels in Algeria— 
that is they agree to pay their own prices for board, 
but it must of course be a losing concern with the 
landlord unless they remunerate him sufficiently for 
the unstinted entertainment he is compelled to lay 
before them. Sometimes at my hotel in Algiers 
when I have asked for any one dish in particular the 
waiters have assured me that it was impossible to 
satisfy my wishes as there would only be just enough 
for the officers, notwithstanding that the dish was 
duly announced on the carte as constituting one of 
the courses of the eveLing meal. 

The Blidah of the present day occupies a different 
site to that on which its predecessor, destroyed by an 
earthquake in 1825, formerly stood, So terrified 
were the inhabitants by the violence of the convulsion 
that they took up their abodes close to the mcuntain 
side, where they founded the present town. Even 
there, however, they found that they had escaped 
one danger only to encounter another, for the fierce 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 277 


Berber tribes made continuous onslaughts from the 
very mountain to which they looked up for protection, 
and committed fearful ravages, retiring in a moment 
beyond the reach of pursuit among the mountain- 
fastnesses known only to themselves. Oranges ripen 
more thoroughly at Blidah than at Algiers, in conse- 
quence of the higher mean temperature, and numerous 
boxes of this fruit as well as of lemons are exported 
to France. By the kind permission of the proprietor 
I went over one of the orange grounds, and nothing 
more “delicious than the shude which those trees af- 
forded from the burning sun, and the fragrance with 
which they filled the listless air, could be conceived. 
The taste, too, of the oranges is rich and sweet, and 
totally different to the unhealthy looking yellow fruit 
which is dignified in England under that name, while 
the rind together with the inner pith are stripped off 
en masse with the slightest pressure, as easily as if 
they had been previously lubricated with oil. Blidah 
has a “Place,” planted with trees and surrounded by 
arcades, a theatre, an hospital, and public gardens 
with a painted dancing booth that savcred most 
curiously of Cremorne, together with a European 
market. There are numerous Moorish houses, and 
two or three Mosques, but :t is needless to say that 
tLe European element predominates. The women of 
Blidah are by no means so spruce as those in Algiers, 
nor is their cogtume so graceful. They wear no yash- 
maks, merely folding the hhaik over the face so as to 
leave’ but one eye visible—a most comfortable practice 
for ladies who through age or accident may have been 
unfortunate enough to lose the other. Hence it is evi- 
dent that a vendor of glass eyes would soon have to shut 


278 THE CORSAIR 


up shop at Blidah as far asthe women are concerned. 

We started about two o’clock in an open carriage 
from our hotel to explore the pass of the Chiffa, the 
entrance to which is about four miles from Blidab. 
Our ‘route lay along a sandy plain running at the foot 
of the Sahel in a south-westerly direction, and after a 
couple of miles driving we crossed a running stream, 
the water of which rose above the axle-trees of our 
carriage. A regiment of Zouaves were going through 
their exercises on the plain, and it was an admirable 
sight to witness the unflagging energy with" which 
they marched at double quick time, with the scorch- 
ing rays of the afternoon sun full upon them, and 
the day so languid that it was almost a fatigue to move 
one’s hand. Riding leisurely with them was the 
vivandiére, while the powerful and thrilling music of 
a military band was cheering them on. In about an 
hour and a half we gained the entrance to the pass, 
which narrowed quickly till the snow-capped summits 
on each side seemed almost to meet in the distant 
perspective. As we advanced farther, the defile as- 
sumed a serpentine form, and so deep a silence reigned 
around that it seemed almost sacrilege to disturb it. 
The path along which we drove was so narrow, too, 
that there would have ‘been barely room for another 
carriage, to pass us, while immediately beneath us 
was a ravine at the bottom of which flowed the river 
from which the gorge. derives its appellation.. The 
road in some places was rugged and uneven, and as 
there was no parapet I shuddered to think of the 
consequences of a false step, especially when the 
lumbering diligence from Medeah drove up and. com- 
yelled us to pass within an inch of the brink” With 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 279 


regard to its solemn beauty, the pass might justly be 
compared to one of those fabled passages which lead 
to the storehouses of Nature, and on which, as a 
meet approach to her adyta, Nature has lavished her 
wildest and most sublime efforts. From the base to 
the hoary summit the mountain sides were strewn 
with the softest verdure, from among which the palm, 
the olive, and the cedar tree reared their heads, while 
ever and anon some vast projecting rock or some 
yawning crevice, literally smothered with the abun- 
dant vegetation, lent a new and fantastic character 
to the scene. Now and then a fonming cascade 
descended in white sheets of spume from the snows 
above, and helped to swell the torrent rushing along 
at our feet, while at other times a tiny waterfall 
trickled musically from a lower elevation, and cooled 
the dry hot air into softness as we passed. In a lonely 
winding, denominated, from the quantity of monkeys 
in its vicinity, the “ Ruisseau des singes,” was a small 
cabaret at which we dismounted and obtained re- 
freshment. How the proprietor manages to make a 
gain out of a small public hotse, situated nuiles away 
from the nearest habitation, and in a part where very 
few Frenchmen care to travel save for business, is 
more than I can understand. Perhaps the solitary 
diligence stops there in its route to the interior and 
the man being of a contented disposition contrives 
to make the proceeds of their custom last for the 
day, and thanks his household gods that the words 
of tlie poet, 
“Oh si angulus ille 
Proximus accedat qui nunc denormat agellum,” 
are mere listless sounds in his eremetical solitude. 


280 THE CORSAIR 


The pass abounds in jackals and apes, the latter 
of which do not emerge from their winter hiding 
places till the snows melt away and the weather 
becomes hotter. The jackals are too cowardly to 
venture out in the day time, but at night they enli- 
ven or terrify the solitary inn-keeper as the case may 
be with their melancholy howlings. Hunger however 
makes them bold, and an instance is related of an old 
woman who lived alone in a cottage by Medeah, and 
who was obliged to bolt the doou for the whole day 
in consequence of a jackal, which prowled round her 
house, waiting till she should come out. Towards 
evening however her unwelcome visitor joined his 
family circle in the Chiffa, and the terrified woman 
was released from her captivity. The pass and the 
greater part of the Sahel is inhabited by gazelles, 
who, during the warmer months, may be seen gam- 
bolling about amid the shadows of the cedar forests. 
The Moors used to tame and make pets of them, but 
the Arabs eat them, and very insipid food they are. 
An eagle wheeled high above our heads as we drove 
along, but soon disappéared, and the only indication 
of life after that was a party of about half a dozen 
Spahis who saluted us as they went by in return for 
our bows. We alighted at a turn in the road wherein 
a stalactite cavern was said to exist, the entrance ‘to 
which was in the side of the ravine. Having had a 
little previous experience, by no means agreeable, in 
exploring caves, I allowed one of my companions to 
take the lead, and we climbed down by means of the 
bushes and fan-palms till we reached a spacious fissure 
into which we entered. The cave was of great height, 
and innumerable stalactites, the growth probably of 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 281 


centuries, hung suspended from the roof, the water 
drops dripping from them like a shower-bath. They 
were too firm to detach, and as there seemed no fur- 
ther food for our curiosity we climbed up again, and 
having entered our carriage drove to within @ few 
miles of Medeah, but ware prevented from advancing 
further on account of the badness of the road, which 
a party of soldiers were engaged in repairing. It 
Was dark when we returned to our hotel at Blidah 
and in consequence" of the paucity of accommodation 
I had ‘a Swedish geotenen quartered in my room 
for the night. 

Next morning after strolling over the town we 
again entered the diligence for Algiers, and whirled 
along the straight endless line of yellow road. This 
time however we branched off towards Koleah, a small 
town built, like Algiers, on the incline of a hill, to 
the left of which, on the summit of another hill, 
Was a semicircular building called by the French the 
‘““Tombeau de la Reine.” By the Arabs however it 
is denominated “K’oubr er Roumiia,” which may 
be translated “ Tomb of the Christian woman,” but 
which, according to Macecarthy, bears a strong resem- 
blance to a Lybian expression signifying “ Royal 
Tomb.” Hence many have ‘conjectured that it was 
the burial place of the Mauritanian Kings—an idea 
which its lofty and at the same time secluded situa- 
tion would tend greatly to strengthen. The building 
itself is about one hundred feet in height, and not 
only have many attempts been made by the Arabs 
to search it for hidden treasure, but the French are 
employed in examining its interior, without as yet 
having obtained the slightest clue to its origin or 


282 THE CORSAIR 


history, both of which sre buried in an obscurity 
as fathomless as that which veils the mysterious 
pyramids. Not far from Constantine a similar build- 
ing exists, though of smaller dimensions, which is 
known as the tomb of Syphax, and which with equal 
certainty might be supposed to be the mausoleum of 
tha ancient Numidian dynasty. The diligence, as we 
arrived at the borders of the Metidja, stuck fast in 6 
rut at the foot’of an incline, and we were obliged to 
_ dismount and walk for about a‘quarter of an hour 
till a more even road presented itself. We arrived 
at Mustapha Superior as the last rays of the setting 
sun were gilding the snow-white cupolas of the pirate- 
city, that lay stretched out in the blue Mediterranean 
like a sleeping denizen of the waters; while the beauty 
of the surrounding scenery presented a strong contrast 
to the sameness and tedium of the unwholesome plain 
that we had just left. 

Stretching across a valley in Mustapha Inferior is 
an aqueduct which ages ago supplied Algiers with 
water. It is composed of eight large Moorish arches 
beneath ‘and seventeen smaller ones above, while a 
pipe of red clay about a foot and a half in diameter, 
through which the water was conveyed, runs along 
the summit. Of the other portions of the pipe all 
traces have been lost, but there is every reason’ to 
believe that ‘he water was procured from the Harash, 
a small river which falls into the sea L2tween five and 
six miles to the eastward of the town. There is no 
clue however as to the date of its erection, ard we 
can only conjecture that it was the work of the Barba- 
rossas who, whatever their tyranny, greatly improved 
the town in the public buildings which they raised

up, and in which so many, remains of the Roman 
Occupation were incorporated. Not far from this 
aqueduct is the present camp in which raw French 
recruits have a taste of tent life, and one which at 
* the same time becomes arduous enough in the fhot- 
ter months when the annual fevers, brought on by 
the miasma of the neighbouring re are at their 
height. 

The principal Roman Catholic Chusch i in Algiers 
is nothing more thai! a Mosque fitted up with the ne- 
cessary appendages of Christian worship. The archi- 
tecture of the interior is the finest in Algiers, but so 
little alteration was originally made in the Moorish 
decorations that the sentence “ There is but one God 
and Mohammed is his prophet” was suffered to re- 
main for a long time in flourishing characters directly 
Over an image of the Virgin. Another Mosque in 
the Bab-el-oued, of far greater benuty though of less 
Size, has also been converted into a Catholic place 
of worship. The scene that presented itself to me 
One day on entering was as pootical as anything I had 
yet seen in Algiers, and at the same time a singular 
combination of heathenism and Christianity. There 
were the carved Moorish arches decorated with gar- 
lands of flowers, and the keblah with a font now 
filling up its once idolized recess. Stained glass had 
been introduced into the apertures through which 
light had formerly been admitted into the Mosque, 
and the colored rays fell pensively upon the stone 
floor as though some bright spirit had taken up its 
abode there to ward off for ever the Infidel crescent. 
Two or three Spanish women in their native mantillas 
were worshipping most devoutly, while a choir of 


284 THE CORSAIR 


youthful voices swelled through the building in har- 
mony with the rolling bass of the organ. A deep 
contrast was formed by the guttural cries from the 
barbarous multitude which saluted my ears as I left 
the building, sacred under the shadow of the cross, ‘ 
though darkened at the sane time by the illusions 
cf Popery. 

The French: seem to have a passion for converting 
Moorish houses into warehouses or private residences, 
adapting them by a few alterations to European tastes 
and requirements. The Museum situated in the Rue 
Lothophage is a noble Moorish pile, spoilt however 
by the glass roof with which the courtyard is covered. 
It is one of the numerous buildings washed by the 
waters of the Mediterranean, and on a stormy day 
the breaking of the waves re-echoes through the 
lower rooms with a melancholy booming cadence. 
There are several valuable wcrks and manuscripts in 
Arabic and Showiah or Berber, over which French 
students sit poring thrice a week, lifting up their 
heads with an indignant “hush” if the footstep of the 
unlucky visitor should call up the slightest sound 
from the marble floor—a by no means unfrequent 
occurrence. The Bishop’s palace, too, is another 
specimen of a metamorphosed Moorish edifice. It 
was formerly the residence of the Algerine Agha, 
and required the incessant exertions of its present 
occupant to prevent its being demoliszed in company 
with other noble buildings. The tracery over the 
arches is as elaborate and fine as lace-work, and sen- 
tences from the Koran are so cunningly incorporated 
that it is difficult at first sight to detect, them from 
the pattern. The doors are of carved and gilt wood, 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 285 


and painted beams run across the ceilings of the 
apartments. The latter are fitted up in extremely 
anti-episcopal style, the carpets being so thick and 
soft that the footstep falls without the slightest noise 
on them, and the chairs, covered with red velvet, 
are of such downy springiness that the very action 
of sitting becomes converted into a strange and novel . 
pleasure. The courtyard is of checquered marble, and 
every window that looks into it is still: protected by 
those jealous cross-bars, which are however painted 
and gil? in keeping with the ether portions of the 
mansion. Outside, this noble edifice presents nothing 
more than four bare walls, and has very much the 
appearance of a whitewashed box; but inside I ques- 
tion whether the Agha with all his luxuries could 
have furnished his rooms in a more comfortable style 
than Monseigneur L’ Eveque d’ Alger. 

The Bishop however, bears an excellent character 
for senctity, for alms-giving, and for his great exer- 
tions in the cause of education. He has a fine and 
imposing presence, and I had the honor of hearing 
him converse for a few moments with an “nglish

@ver exercise themselves in those manly and athletic

conspicuous. Not far from the school, on the summit

driven from his country: in consequence of his political 
opinions, and not even allowed to reside or sleep for 
a single night in the town without the permission 
of the police. I paid him a visit one day, and must

regretted. The approach to his grounds led through 
several walkc, over which the foliage of numerous 
trees planted in the time of the Deys threw an agree- 
able shade, while the air, on a nearer approach to his 
residence, was heavy with the mingled odours of the 
exotics which carpeted every inch of the ground. 
Almost every tropical tree or plant grew in his gar- 
den, so that he was enabled to enjoy not only the 
necessaries, but the actual luxuries of life, which 
offered themselves for the mere trouble of gathering. 
He made his own wine, kept his own poultry, killed 
his own meat, and baked his own bread, and the 
healthy color on the cheeks of his domestics bore 
strong testimony to the purity of the mountain air. 
The idva of an exile is generally connected by us 
either with the barren solitude of a Patmos or a St. 
Helena, and I only wish that every deserving patriot 
had as good and agrevable an abode as the proscribed 
exile of France. The “banished” was out for a walk 
when I called, so that I had not the pleasure of seeing 
him, but took away instead a magnificent bouquet, 
culled for me as a souvenir by one of the rosy-cheeked 
damsels. I suppose he has now returned, in,conse- 
quence of the general amnesty, to the less picturesque 
shores of his native France. P

ner. Ata turn in the road a huge, mastiff spreng 


out, and seemed uncertain as to which of the two he -

them v@ stick, and to throw a stone at the animal 
would at once have provoked him to spring on the 
thrower without further delay. In this emergency a 
brilliant idea presented itself. They seized each a 
handful of stones and stood back to back, resolving 
that if the dog sprung on A, B should instantly - 
assail him with a stone so as to engage his attention 
to himself, and that jvhile the dog was rushing to- 
wards B, A should in turn discharge a well-aimed 
Missile at him, so as to turn his attention towards 
himself again, and in this manner at any rate to keep 
him at bay till the greater portion of his strength 
was exhausted. The proprietor, however, came run- 
hing up in a few moments and called off tho dog, 
admiring greatly the ingenijus manner in which 
they had saved themselves from an inevitable bite. 

I must not conclude this chapter without mention- 
ing a series of strange reports that floated about 
Algiers shortly ,before my departure, and which it is 
no fiction to say have to a certain degree found their 
realization in the late border warfare with Morocco. 
Although the French were not willing to admit it, 
yet a rumour, from being first whispered in odd

nooks and corners was spread gradually over the 
town, to the effect that a rising of the natives was 
confidently expected. Not only in Algiers itself, 
but in all the principal towns, the idea was fast 
gaining ground, and in Oran a few days later I found | 
that it had assumed a more tangible form, several 
particulars being even given as to the mode in which 
it was to take place. The head quarters of the revolt 
were to be at Mostaganem, and thither on a’certain 
day numerous Kabyle tribes from the interior were to 
repair, without a more ostensible purpose thin that 
of commerce, while gunpowder and arms were to be 
conveyed to them through the frontier of Morocco by 
their unconquered Kabyle brethren on the other side. 
There seems little doubt that the attack on the French 
by the Moroqueens was intended as a prelude to’ the 
introduction of ammunition, which unless the French _ 
outposts were removed would of course pave been an 
impossibility. Happily the numerous sorties of the 
Moroqueens have been as yet without effect, and 
have resulted in the loss to them of a portion of their 
territory: but it is not too much to say that had they 
gained an advantage of any importance the whole of 
Algeria would by this time have been the unhappy 
scene of a mutiny. «In the town of Algiers the

Most of the French writers seem to have a hazy 
idea that France ought te stand forth proudly pre- 
eminent among European naxions, on account of the 
emancipation from individual thraldom which the 
indigenous population has received at her hands. 
The Moors, they argue, have been freed from the 
despotism of the Turks, the Negroes have been libe- 
rated, and the Jews, no longer insulted and oppressed, 
are the most flourishing and wealthy of all the com- 
munity.» By the decrees of 14th July 1850 indigenous 
schools have been instituted, both for boys and girls,

CHAPTER XI. 


The conveyance by sea from the eastward to the 
westward, along the coast, is of the most wretched 
and uncomfortable description that can well be con- 
ceived. There is no regular line of steamers, and 


the only way in which it is possible to travel is by 


engaging places on board one of the coast-guard 
vessels which sails every ten days. The principal 
berths are of course occupied by the naval officers 
in command of the ship, and six small boxes with 
hard mattresses, and no bed clothes on them, are left 
at the disposal of any military officers whose duty 
or inclination may oblige them to adopt that route. 
If these berths should happen to be disengaged, the 
tourist hes a chance of securing’ one by presenting 
himself at the post office at six o’clock on the morn- 
ing of departure; if not, he must think himself lucky 
if he can procure a place on the deck. The truth is, 


_that it is considered a favor to take passengers at all, 


and the naval officers take pretty good care by their 
contemptuous conduct to make you fully aware of 


’ the fact. 


At eight o’clock on a lovely evening, a day or two 
a‘ter the Bairam, I embarked in company with an 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. ~ 295” 


English lady and two gentlemen on board the steamer 

that was bound for Oran. The lady was fortunate 

enough—if fortunate it may be termed—to secure a 
berth, while the two gentlemen and myself had to 
take up our quarters, along with the Iuggags and 
riff-raff, on the unswahbel planks. I had seldom 
seen Algiers so calmly and solemnly beautiful as ‘t 
appeared from the ship the momert before our de- 
parture. The waning moon, crescent as the symbol 
of Byzantium, was tinging the white cupolas and 
minarets with her silvery rays, while the stars were 
gleaming with their usual magnificence from the 
canopy of dark azure for which the Algerine nights 
are so especially famous. The ship was crowded with 
the lower orders of Frenchmen, a sprinkling of Arabs, 

and a few Jews, and as every one on their arrival 
spread out mattresses and blankets, and ensconced 
themselves therein without deloy, the tout ensemble 
was as uncomfortably picturesque as a rainy walking 
tour among the highlands. Not feeling disposed. to 
retire at so early,an hour as half-past eight we en- 

deavored to walk up | and down the decks,” but were 
obliged to desist in consequence of the number of 
sleepy heads and legs against 1 which ‘we accidentally 
stumbled in the darkness, andl the volley of “sacrés” 

which followed us in consequence About nine o'clock 
we began to steam gently out of the-bay, keeping.

pelt'down in drenching josrantas ’T crawled .unper- 

ceived into the common sailor’s berth, where about

Congratulating myself or my good fortune in being 
able to get under cover during the storm, I stretched 
myself on the floor and was soon in a comfortable 
doze, when a rough hand laid on my shoulder awoke 
me, and a voice demanded “Qui est ce?” “C'est 
moi,” I answered sleepily, tuzning on the other side. 
“You must walk!” said the sailor. “Is there no 
other place of shelter in this rain?” I asked rather 
indignantly. “Yes,” he replied with a malicious 
chuckle, “There’s the deck!” 

So I was obliged to give up my dry quorters and 
mount again on deck. The rain was pouring down 
more vehemently than ever, and the lightning was 
flashing angrily from all sides of the heavens. How- 
ever there was no help for it, so having dragged a 
tarpauling from an adjacent heap of luggage I rolled 
myself therein and managed to sleep very comforta- 
bly for two or three Louys, till I was awoke by a vague 
sensation of cold, and had the pleasure of finding that 
I was wet all over, the rain having penetrated my 
covering. It is impossible to describe the grandeur 
of the storm at this period, every peal of thunder 
crashing and reverberating among the coast moun- 
tains on our left, and seeming to leap, like a live 
thing, from peak to peak, till it died away in the far 
distance, in ten thousand fainter echoes. I got up 
and groped my way to the cabin in which slept the 
half-dozen favored occupants of the passenger berths, 
and a more miserable place could not well be con- 
ceived. The “boxes”—for they were nothing more— 
formed a semicircle, the extremities of which coincided 
with those of the side, at which I entered, and the 
radii of which would be scarcely more than five feet. 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 297” 
A rough wooden table and a few chairs constituted 
the whole furniture, and I was very sorry to see the 
English lady as she lay there, with nothing but 
greasy-looking Frenchmen around, and an insuffer- 
ably close smell from the rancid oil of the lamps. 
However I crawled under the table, which, like that 
of the imprisoned cobbler in Pickwick, served me ws 
an excellent four-post bedstead, and passed the rest 
of the night in comparative comfort, maugre my wet 
clothes. The day dawned softly and beautifully, 
tinging with innumerable colors-the fantastic coast- 
. ine, in which mountain rose behind mountain assu- 
ming forms the most picturesque it is possible to 
conceive, and covered to the very summit with the 
most brilliant verdure. In almost every instance 
their bases were thickly wooded, and sprinkled every 
now and then with white Moorish houses or furreted 
Marabouts, the purity of whose zolor rendered them 
far conspicuous. In a delicious valley, upon whose 
emerald carpeting the rays of the early morning 
sun fell with an inexpressibly glad effect, and which 
imaged forth strongly the idea of the fabled “happy 
valley,” stretched a Roman aqueduct, of which, as 
our vessel was not more than three or four hundred 
yards from the shore, I had an excellent view. This 
was the aqueduct most probably that supplied the 
ueighbouring town of .Cherchell, to waich we were 
fast approachizg, with water; and a little beyond it, 
in a circle of wood, are the remains of a Roman bath- 
house. We dropped anchor for upwards of an hour 
opposite Cherchell—the Phoonician Jol, and the later 
Julia Cessarea—the seat of the chief bishopric of the® 
early African church. The modern town is however

298 THE CORSAIR 


almost entirely French, very little even of the Moorish 
element remaining, and that little consisting of a few 
Marabouts, which are, strictly speaking, outside the 
walls. There are the remains of a Moorish fort, 
constructed originally of stones taken from Roman 
buildings, part of which is. swallowed up by the 
Mediterranean—the encroachments of which upon 
certain parts of its coast have been at different times 
very great. Julia Cosarea affords an excellent illus- 
tration of this, for its classic ruins gleam for from 
the shore, under the blue water, like the spectral 
palaces of Lough Neagh, and prove at the same time . 
that it must have been one of the most flourishing 
cities along the coast. The market-place of Cherchell 
abounds in Roman remains, and numerous statuettes 
and capitals are frequently unearthed at the first turn 
of the iabourer’s spade. Nor is there a lack of more 
valuable treasure, for the Arak and Frerich boys are 
constantly bringing to light jewelled rings, coins, and 
gold and silver ornaments of great value, for which 
Government affords them an adequate remuneration. 
Many Christian tombs also have been excavated, and 
to the left of the city is a Christian cemetery of which 
almost every tombstone is as fresh and uninjured as - 
when the chisel of the sculptor first vibrated over it. 

Again we heaved anchor, and sped on our sunlit 
way. The word “sped” is however hardly applicable 
to the rate at which our good ship wes cleaving the 
water. She was a paddle-wheel corvette of four guns, 
built originally to convey troops to the Crimea. “With 
her engines however at full speed, and an occasional 
display of canvas, it will hardly be credited -that we 
averaged the respectable rate of from two to two and

a half knots the hour, only once during the voyage

extreme smallness of the paddle-wheels in comparison 
with her size, but at all events it was no matter of

the two days’ voyage till we dropped anchor opposite 
Oran. It seemed as if they strove to hide the very 
sight of the sea, as though it were some hideous 
phantom, worse even than that very sickness which 
indicated its presence. As the last stroke of the 
engine ceased to vibrate opposite Mers-el-Kebir they 
came forth, the arms of the fumales covered, as usual, 
with bracelets, and their faces dirty with the grime 
of eight and forty hours. The little girl was crying 
and sobbing 9s if her heart would break, and uttering 
ever and anon the doleful and unnatural ery of “lu, 
lu, lu!” such as the Moorish women uttered at the 
festival of the Aissoua, and which it appears is a’cry 
of joy or grief according to circumstances.

when it does oceur it is generally warm and languid,

dignified, and called in a pompous voice for soup,

It is impossible to describe the grand and*fantastic

immediately dispersed to their different occupations.

We next touched at Tenes, a rather picturesque

From being a simple military port in 1837 it began

eighteen hundred hectares; and the moment this

Another attempt to re-animate this body without a

Strolling through Oran one fine morning, with the

command over his horse, with such powerful auxiliaries 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 313 is 


‘as that combination of spur and bit presents. So 
great is the leverage exefcised that the jaw of thé 
animal can actually be broken off in a moment, 
should he become unmanageable. The saddle, too, 
is raised three or four inches before and behind, and

be by no means easy té fafl off. This circumstance 
is greatly in favor of their mode of warfare, with 
which the French were very much fasassed, previous 
to the capture of AJgiers. The enemy rode in circles, 
each man taking his aim as he passed a certain point, 
and immediately lying forward when he had fired, 
reloading his gun as he galloped round in time for 
the next discharge, so that by that means a constant 
and hurtful stream of fire was kept up, which, issuing 
from all quarters, did infinitely more damage than a 
succession of regular volleys, And even in the faces 
of the generally b/asé and ennuyé French inhabitants 
admiration at this noble band sefmed to sparkle forth 
ag they issued every man from his shop or cabaret to 
gaze on the warlike descendants of Akhbar sweeping, 
majestically by.

CHAPTER XII. 


The prospect of a by no means unpleasant passage 
presented itself as I stepped on board the “Titan”- 
corvette, whose clean white decks and “taut” ap- 
pearance formed a most delightful contrast to the 
dirty, unswabbed, and slovenly aspect which charac- 
terized the tub in which we had been conveyed to 
Oran. A large awning, too, had been stretched over 
the quarter-deck, beneath which a couple of ladies 
were seated embroidering. Tliese were the wife and 
daughter of the Capfain, who was polite enough to 
ask us to join his family circle—an honor that we 

"Ytre the more able to appreciate, as, according to a 
notice conspicuously displayed on all the coast-guard 
ships, the quarter-decks were reserved solely for the 
use of the officers in command, none of the ordinary 
passengers being permitted to mount thereon. ° 

We still kept close to the shore, and the rugged 
grouping of the mountains with their alternations of 
light and shade yielded not a whit in Rauty to those 
which had previously greeted us on our journey to 
Orap. The sun went down in a perfect flood of 
crimson glory, and the peaked tips of the smal] island 
to our right seemed to retain as it were the gilded 


AND HIS CONQUEROR. 317 


tays long after the luminary had disappeared below. 
the horizon. And then came night, soft and beauti- 
ful, with its myriad chromatic stars, and the giant 
mountains, so lately bathed in the warm sunshine, 
stood forth anew silvered with the tremulous beams 
of eternal Cynthia. , Tlrere’was something solemn in 
the stillness of that night. Not a sqund to break the 
deep silence around save the almostanusical plashing 
of the paddle-wheels as they cleft in alternate strokes 
the listless waters. ° A slight accident to one of the 
floats detained us at anchor for upwards of an hour, 
and then, when the last vibrating stroke of the engine 
had died away, Nature seemed literally to have been 
rocked to sleep. 

In no waters, I believe, is the peculiar phenomenon, 
termed phosphorescent light, displayed to such advan-

While breakfasting off a beefsteak—not the small 
ounce of meat to which the French sobriquet of 
“bifteck” corresponds, but an actual solid half-pound 
of carnal food—I heard the approaching strains of a 
military band. Thé tune was “The girl I left behind 
me,” and I think I may be excused for rushing like 
a child to the window, and watching the gallant 
fellows till the echo of their reverberating footsteps, 
distinguishable even above the inspired brass, died 
away in the warm distance. Who could be insensible 
to the endearing associations which that tune, so truly 
and essentially British, and associated with the gilded 
hours of our unfledged infatiey, and the maturer aspi- 
rations of our genial manhood must ever call forth P 
And the man who can receive without emotion its 
vivid inspirations must be almost as cold a patriot as 
he who refuses to shew a proper respect to the solemn 
hymn-tones of our in*mitable.Netional Anthem.
